An aperiodic set of Wang tiles for every quadratic irrational
Jarkko Kari and Sébastien Labbé and Pieter Mostert

Abstract
We propose a sufficient condition for the non-periodicity of a set of Wang tiles. It applies to sets of Wang tiles whose tiles have vertical or horizontal stripes. The proof is based on a geometric argument involving a quadrilateral circumscribed to a parabola from which we conclude the irrationality of the densities of the vertical and horizontal stripes. We apply the sufficient condition to propose new proofs of non-periodicity of known sets of Wang tiles, including an encoding of Penrose tilings into 24 Wang tiles and the family of metallic mean Wang tiles. Conversely, for every pair ( α , β ) ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] 2 (\alpha,\beta)\in[0,1]^{2} of irrational numbers in the same quadratic number field, we construct a finite aperiodic set of Wang tiles with stripes that admits a valid tiling whose density of vertical stripes is α \alpha and density of horizontal stripes is β \beta . Keywords: aperiodic tilings, Wang tiles, Diophantine quadratic equation, quadrilateral, parabola. MSC 2020: Primary 52C23; Secondary 37B51, 11D09, 51N20.



1
Introduction

A tiling of the plane by a set of tiles is a covering of the plane by isometric
copies of the tiles that do not intersect except maybe on their boundary. The
description of the tilings of the plane allowed by a finite set
of tiles is a rich subject within geometry
[
GS87
]
with strong connections
to dynamical systems
[
Fog02
]
,
number theory
[
BR10
]
and
computability
[
Rob71
]
. In physics, it has direct applications to the theory of quasicrystals

[
Sen95
]
and aperiodic order
[
BG13
]
.
We say that a set of tiles is
aperiodic
when two conditions are satisfied:
(
existence
) there exists one tiling of the plane by the tiles and
(
non-periodicity
) none of the tilings are periodic, i.e., invariant under a non-trivial translation.
Proving that a set of tiles is aperiodic is difficult problem,
because proving existence is undecidable in general
[
Ber66
]
.

One strategy to prove that a set of tiles is aperiodic involves the presence of
a hierarchical structure in the tilings allowed and enforced by the tiles.
A hierarchical structure is described as a substitution rule allowing to construct larger
and larger patches and tilings at the limit, thus proving existence.
If the hierarchical decomposition is uniquely defined, it also proves non-periodicity.
It is also sometimes called the
unique composition property

[
AGS92
]
.
This is an old strategy
[
Pen74
]
which is still used nowadays

[
Aki12
,
SMKGS24
]
. But it often results in a long and technical argument, as
it entails the verification of many combinatorial cases.

A promising approach was proposed in

[
Kar96
]
where a short proof of non-periodicity was deduced from
multiplicative arithmetic and the fundamental theorem of arithmetics.
Basically, non-periodicity is deduced from the fact that
the only integer solution to the equation
2^{k}3^{\ell}=1
is
k=\ell=0
.
This argument was used in
[
Cul96
]
to design an aperiodic set of 13 Wang tiles, which remained the smallest known such set until the discovery of the 11 tile set and the proof of its minimality by Jeandel and Rao
[
JR21
]
. The argument was also used in
[
ENP07
]
to cover a slightly more general family
using integers other than
2
or
3
.
But, such short arguments for proving non-periodicity remain elusive.

In this article, we propose a new proof of non-periodicity of sets of tiles
based on additive arithmetics.
The method works for certain sets of Wang tiles.
Recall that a Wang tile is a unit square with labelled edges.
Given a finite set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}
, a
tiling of the plane by translated copies of the tiles is
valid

if the label of the common edge of adjacent tiles is the same.
We represent a valid tiling as a map
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathcal{T}
called a
configuration
.
The set
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
of valid configurations defines what is called a
subshift

which is closed for the prodiscrete topology and for the
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-action induced by the shift.
A subshift
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
is said to be
aperiodic
if it is non-empty and if every
configuration in
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
is non-periodic.
As mentioned above, proving that a subshift of finite type is non-empty is
undecidable
[
Ber66
]
and proving that it is non-periodic is also
difficult.

The method proposed in this article works for sets of Wang tiles with stripes.
See the precise definition in Section
2.4
.
Sets of Wang tiles with stripes include many examples, the simplest non-trivial one
being the Ammann set of 16 Wang tiles
[
GS87
]
.
We will use this example to illustrate, in the remainder of the
introduction, the main results of this article.
We say that the Ammann set
\mathcal{T}
of 16 Wang tiles
has stripes because it can be partitioned into four non-empty subsets

\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{\square}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}

as follows:

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right\}.

The alphabet of the labels of horizontal sides and vertical sides of
the Ammann set of 16 Wang tiles is
\{1,2,3,4,5,6\}
.
We may observe that for every pair
(u,v)
of labels of opposite edges
we have
u\in\{1,2\}
if and only if
v\in\{1,2\}

and
u\in\{3,4,5,6\}
if and only if
v\in\{3,4,5,6\}
.
Thus, we associate the subset
\{3,4,5,6\}
with the presence of a horizontal or vertical stripe
and the subset
\{1,2\}
with the absence of such a stripe.
Tilings by Wang tiles with stripes are naturally divided into
rows and columns with or without stripes, see Figure
1
. Note that the
tile sets by Kari
[
Kar96
]
and Culik
[
Cul96
]
have analogous horizontal stripes but no vertical stripes. Our proofs essentially use stripes in both directions.

Figure 1:
A tiling of a
10\times 5
rectangle with the Ammann set of 16 Wang tiles.
The tiling is made of rows and columns with or without stripes
according to the partition
\{1,2\}\cup\{3,4,5,6\}
of the
alphabet of labels.
The density of these stripes in a valid tiling
of the plane is
\varphi^{-1}
where
\varphi
is the golden ratio.

A new criteria for non-periodicity

Our first result proposes a sufficient condition for the density of vertical
and horizontal stripes to be the solution of a system of quadratic equations.
We deduce non-periodicity of the set of tilings when the solutions to the
system are irrational. The sufficient condition depends on a quadrilateral
in the plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
that is determined by a set of Wang tiles with stripes.
Here is what it means for the Ammann set.
Suppose that we map the vertical labels
and the horizontal labels to vectors as follows:

\displaystyle\textsc{v}:1\mapsto\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\quad 2\mapsto\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\quad 3\mapsto\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\quad 4\mapsto\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\quad 5\mapsto\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\quad 6\mapsto\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right),

\displaystyle\textsc{h}:1\mapsto\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\quad 2\mapsto\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\quad 3\mapsto\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\quad 4\mapsto\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\quad 5\mapsto\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\quad 6\mapsto\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right).

For every tile

\tau=\hbox to23.42pt{\vbox to25.56pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 1.51349pt\lower-2.82193pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{13.29057pt}{7.80565pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.81259pt}{12.4613pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{1.81952pt}{7.80565pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.35599pt}{0.51108pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\in\mathcal{T}
, we observe that

\textsc{v}(a)+\textsc{h}(b)-\textsc{v}(c)-\textsc{h}(d)

takes only 4 distinct values
and the value depends only on the presence or absence of the stripes.
More precisely, for every tile
\tau\in\mathcal{T}
, we have

\textsc{v}(a)+\textsc{h}(b)-\textsc{v}(c)-\textsc{h}(d)=\begin{cases}A&\text{ if }\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\square},\\
B&\text{ if }\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus},\\
C&\text{ if }\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar},\\
D&\text{ if }\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus},\end{cases}

where

A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\quad B=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\quad C=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\quad\text{ and }\quad D=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right).

We see the points
A
,
B
,
D
and
C
(in that particular order) as the
consecutive vertices of a quadrilateral in the plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Note that the quadrilateral can be simple (not self-intersecting) or complex
(self-intersecting).
When the equation above holds for every tile, we say that the set
\mathcal{T}
of Wang tiles
with stripes
determines the quadrilateral
ABDC

(see Definition
3.1
).
In this situation, we prove the following result.

Theorem A
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a set of Wang tiles
having horizontal and vertical stripes with partition

\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{\square}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{T}
determines the quadrilateral
ABDC
for some integer points

A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}\\
A_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),B=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}\\
B_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),C=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C_{1}\\
C_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),D=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}D_{1}\\
D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
If

\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}&D_{1}\\
A_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=0,\qquad\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}&D_{1}\\
B_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=-\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C_{1}&D_{1}\\
C_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\neq 0

and there exists
i\in\{1,2\}
such that
(B_{i}+C_{i})^{2}-4A_{i}D_{i}>0
is not a perfect square,
then the set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}
admits no periodic tiling.

We observe that the hypotheses of Theorem
A
are satisfied
for the Ammann set of 16 Wang tiles presented above
with
(B_{1}+C_{1})^{2}-4A_{1}D_{1}=5
. Thus, it provides a new proof that the Ammann set
of Wang tiles admits no periodic tiling, without using the classical argument
based on unique decomposition property or self-similarity.
Also, Theorem
A

provides a positive answer to Question 2 in
[
Lab25b
]

which was asking if one can directly prove that a Wang shift is non-periodic
using equations satisfied by labels of Wang tiles given by integer vectors
following the short arithmetical argument for the non-periodicity of Kari’s
tile set.

The proof of Theorem
A
is short and is done in
Section
3
. In Section
4
,
we apply Theorem
A
to obtain new proofs of non-periodicity of
many known sets of Wang tiles including the encoding of Penrose tilings into 24 Wang tiles
and the recent family of metallic mean Wang tiles.
We also propose an algorithm based on linear algebra
to find the quadrilateral determined by a set of Wang tiles with stripes.
This is done in Section
5
.

Stripe densities are solutions to a quadratic equation

y=\varphi x
y=-\varphi^{-1}x
A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
B=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
C=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
D=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
O
R
S
P
Q
parabola
x=\frac{1}{4}(y-1)^{2}+1

Figure 2:

The Ammann set of 16 Wang tiles determines the quadrilateral
ABDC
with

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}&B_{1}&C_{1}&D_{1}\\
A_{2}&B_{2}&C_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0&1&-1\\
0&1&-1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
The parabola tangent to the four lines supported by the sides of the
quadrilateral
ABDC

has equation
x=\frac{1}{4}(y-1)^{2}+1
.
The density of tiles with stripes in a tiling by Ammann tiles
satisfy a system of Diophantine equations whose solutions
are represented by the two lines passing through the origin
O

tangent to the parabola.
The slope of these tangent lines are irrational
from which we deduce the non-periodicity of the Ammann set of Wang tiles.
One may interact with this figure at

https://www.geogebra.org/m/a4vmxgft
.

The proof of Theorem
A
is based on an elegant equation
satisfied by the densities of vertical and horizontal stripes.
Suppose that the vertical stripes (
i.e.
, tiles in
\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
) have density
\alpha
, and that the horizontal stripes (tiles in

\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
) have density
\beta
.
We show in Lemma
3.2

for every doubly-periodic configuration
(and in Proposition
7.1

for all configurations),
that the densities satisfy the following equation:

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)=(1-\alpha)\left((1-\beta)A+\beta B\right)+\alpha\left((1-\beta)C+\beta D\right).

(1.1)

Therefore, if the quadrilateral
ABDC
determined by a set of Wang tiles

\mathcal{T}
is such that the solutions to Equation (
1.1
)
are quadratic irrationals, we conclude that the set
\mathcal{T}
admits no periodic
tiling.
This is how Theorem
A
is proved.

The solutions to Equation (
1.1
)
are studied in detail in Section
6
.
Assuming that the quadrilateral
ABDC
is in a generic position,
where consecutive and opposite sides are not parallel,
it is known that there exists a unique parabola tangent
to the four lines defined by the sides of the quadrilateral
ABDC

[
Mus37
]
.
The right hand side of Equation (
1.1
)
describes a set of tangent lines to this parabola.
Thus, a solution
(\alpha,\beta)
corresponds to a tangent line
passing through the origin
O
.
For example, for the quadrilateral
ABDC

determined above by the Ammann set of Wang tiles,
we find that the parabola tangent to the four lines supported by the sides
of the quadrilateral has equation
x=\frac{1}{4}(y-1)^{2}+1
,
see Figure
2
.
One of the two tangent lines passing through the origin
intersects the segments
AB
and
CD
of the quadrilateral in the points

R=(1-\beta)A+\beta B
and

S=(1-\beta)C+\beta D
for some
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
.
The other tangent line to the parabola passing through the origin
intersects the segments
AC
and
BD
of the quadrilateral
in the points

P=(1-\alpha)A+\alpha C
and

Q=(1-\alpha)B+\alpha D
for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
.
We may recover the value of
\alpha
and
\beta
from this geometric construction
because the points
P
,
Q
,
R
and
S
satisfy

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)=O=(1-\beta)P+\beta Q=(1-\alpha)R+\alpha S

(1.2)

when
\alpha=\beta=\varphi^{-1}

where
\varphi
is the golden ratio.
We observe that Equation (
1.2
)
is equivalent to Equation (
1.1
).

The solutions to the equation for every quadrilateral
ABDC

are described very precisely in
Proposition
6.2
.
Also, in Lemma
6.3
,
we show that for every pair
(\alpha,\beta)
of elements in the
same quadratic number field, there exist four points
A,B,C,D\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such
that
(\alpha,\beta)
is a solution to Equation (
1.1
).

A set of Wang tiles for every pair of quadratic numbers

Many aperiodic sets of tiles are related to the golden ratio:
Penrose tiles
[
Pen74
]
,
Ammann tiles
[
GS87
]
,
Jeandel-Rao tiles
[
JR21
]
and
the Hat tile
[
SMKGS24
]
, just to list a few.
Aperiodic sets of tiles exists whose structure is explained by other algebraic numbers

[
Fre17
]
or even non-alebraic
[
FGMn24
]
but examples remain sparse.
Recently, a new family of aperiodic Wang tiles was introduced whose tilings are non-periodic
and associated with metallic means
[
Lab25a
,
Lab25b
]
.
For every integer
n\geq 1
, the
n
-th metallic mean is the positive root

\beta
of the polynomial
x^{2}-nx-1
.
Tilings with sets of Wang tiles from this family have horizontal and vertical stripes
and the density of these stripes are equal to the inverse of
\beta
.

Question 1.1
.

For which vertical and horizontal stripe densities can we construct an

as simple as possible
set of Wang tiles with stripes whose tilings
achieve these densities?

Other questions like these were listed in the last section of
[
Lab25b
]
.
Note that there are partial answers to that question.
Indeed, for every two-dimensional (non-uniform) rectangular substitution,
we know there exists a general construction of a set of Wang tiles
whose set of valid configurations are representatives of configurations
obtained by the substitution rule
[
Moz89
]
.
Considering Mozes’ construction for direct products of two distinct
one-dimensional substitutions already give lots of examples.
But, the construction proposed by Mozes is complicated as there is a lot of
information stored in each Wang tile. As a consequence,
the construction is very hardly used in practice.
Here, as in the family of metallic mean Wang tiles, we are interested in
solutions which are as simple as possible.

In this article, we provide a simple constructive answer to
Question
1.1
for algebraic numbers of
degree 2.
Namely, for every pair
(\alpha,\beta)\in[0,1]^{2}
of elements in the same quadratic number field
such that both are rational or irrational,
we construct a finite set of Wang tiles with stripes whose
density of vertical stripes is
\alpha
and
density of horizontal stripes is
\beta
.

Theorem B
.

Let
(\alpha,\beta)\in[0,1]^{2}

be a pair of elements of a quadratic number field
K

such that

\alpha
and
\beta
are both irrational or both rational.
There exists a finite set

\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{\square}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}

of Wang tiles with vertical and horizontal stripes
and there exist four non-collinear points
A,B,C,D\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}

such that

(i)

(\alpha,\beta)
is a solution to Equation (
1.1
),

(ii)

\mathcal{T}
determines the quadrilateral
ABDC

whose opposite sides are not parallel,

(iii)

{\cal T}
admits a tiling
w:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\longrightarrow{\cal T}

where vertical stripes (
i.e.
, tiles in
\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
) have density
\alpha
, and the horizontal stripes
(tiles in
\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
) have density
\beta
,
and

(iv)

in every valid tiling by
\mathcal{T}
, the horizontal and the
vertical densities of stripes exist and are solutions of
Equation (
1.1
).

In particular, if
\alpha
and
\beta
are irrational, then the tile set
\mathcal{T}
is aperiodic.

Theorem
B
is proved in
Section
8

based on results proved in
Section
6

and Section
7
.

Structure of the article

In Section
2
, we recall the notion
of Wang tiles and present some specific notation used in this article.
In Section
3
, we prove
Theorem
A
, that is, we present a short argument for
concluding the non-periodicity of a set of Wang tiles with stripes provided
some sufficient condition is satisfied.
In Section
4
,
we apply the sufficient condition to propose
new proofs of non-periodicity of known sets of Wang tiles, including
an encoding of Penrose tilings into 24 Wang tiles and the family of metallic mean Wang tiles.
In Section
5
, we present a method based on linear
algebra to compute a quadrilateral determined by a set of Wang tiles with
vertical and horizontal stripes if one exists.
We use this method to give new proofs of non-periodicity for three additional
known aperiodic sets of Wang tiles.
In Section
6
,
we present a complete description of the set of solutions to
Equation (
1.1
).
In Section
7
,
we show that densities of stripes in valid
configurations over a set of tiles determining a quadrilateral
ABDC
must
exist and be a solution to Equation (
1.1
).
In Section
8
, we prove
Theorem
B
. We conclude with some open questions in Section
9
.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Topological dynamical systems

Most of the notions introduced here can be found in
[
Wal82
]
.
A
dynamical system
is
a triple
(X,G,T)
, where
X
is a topological space,
G
is a topological
group and
T
is a continuous function
G\times X\to X
defining a left action
of
G
on
X
:
if
x\in X
,
e
is the identity element of
G
and
g,h\in G
, then using
additive notation for the operation in
G
we have
T(e,x)=x

and
T(g+h,x)=T(g,T(h,x))
.
In other words, if one denotes the transformation
x\mapsto T(g,x)

by
T^{g}
, then
T^{g+h}=T^{g}T^{h}
.
In this work, we consider the Abelian group
G=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
.

If
Y\subset X
, let
\overline{Y}
denote the topological closure of
Y
and
let
\overline{Y}^{T}:=\cup_{g\in G}T^{g}(Y)
denote the
T
-closure of
Y
.
A subset
Y\subset X
is
T
-invariant
if
\overline{Y}^{T}=Y
.
A dynamical system
(X,G,T)
is called
minimal
if
X
does
not contain any nonempty, proper, closed
T
-invariant subset.
The left action of
G
on
X
is
free

if
g=e
whenever there exists
x\in X
such that
T^{g}(x)=x
.

Let
(X,G,T)
and
(Y,G,S)
be two dynamical systems with
the same topological group
G
.
A
homomorphism

\theta:(X,G,T)\to(Y,G,S)
is a continuous
function
\theta:X\to Y
satisfying the commuting property
that
S^{g}\circ\theta=\theta\circ T^{g}
for every
g\in G
.
A homomorphism
\theta:(X,G,T)\to(Y,G,S)
is called an
embedding

if it is one-to-one, a
factor map
if it is onto, and a
topological
conjugacy
if it is both one-to-one and onto and its inverse map is continuous.
If
\theta:(X,G,T)\to(Y,G,S)
is a factor map,
then
(Y,G,S)
is called a
factor
of
(X,G,T)

and
(X,G,T)
is called an
extension
of
(Y,G,S)
.
Two dynamical systems are
topologically conjugate
if there is a
topological conjugacy between them.

2.2
Subshifts and shifts of finite type

In this section, we introduce multidimensional subshifts,
a particular type of dynamical system

[
LM95
, §13.10]
,

[
Sch01
,
Lin04
,
Hoc16
]
.
Let
\mathcal{A}
be a finite set,
d\geq 1
, and let
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
be the set of all maps

x:\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to\mathcal{A}
, equipped with the compact product topology.
An element
x\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is called a
configuration

and we write it as
x=(x_{\boldsymbol{m}})=(x_{\boldsymbol{m}}:{\boldsymbol{m}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d})
,
where
x_{\boldsymbol{m}}\in\mathcal{A}
denotes the value of
x
at
{\boldsymbol{m}}
.
The topology on
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is compatible with the metric defined for all
configurations
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
by
\dist(x,x^{\prime})=2^{-\min\left\{\|{\boldsymbol{n}}\|\,:\,x_{\boldsymbol{n}}\neq x^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{n}}\right\}}

where
\|{\boldsymbol{n}}\|=|n_{1}|+\dots+|n_{d}|
.
The
shift action

\sigma:{\boldsymbol{n}}\mapsto\sigma^{\boldsymbol{n}}
of the additive group
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
on
\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is defined by

(\sigma^{\boldsymbol{n}}(x))_{\boldsymbol{m}}=x_{{\boldsymbol{m}}+{\boldsymbol{n}}}

(2.1)

for every
x=(x_{\boldsymbol{m}})\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
and
{\boldsymbol{n}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
If
X\subset\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
,
let
\overline{X}
denote the topological closure of
X

and let
{\overline{X}^{\sigma}}:=\{\sigma^{\boldsymbol{n}}(x)\mid x\in X,{\boldsymbol{n}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\}

denote the shift-closure of
X
.
A subset
X\subset\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is
shift-invariant
if

{\overline{X}^{\sigma}}=X
. A closed, shift-invariant subset

X\subset\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is a
subshift
.
If
X\subset\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is a subshift we write

\sigma=\sigma^{X}
for the restriction of the shift action
(
2.1
) to
X
.
When
X
is a subshift,
the triple
(X,\mathbb{Z}^{d},\sigma)
is a dynamical system
and the notions presented in the previous section hold.

A configuration
x\in X
is
periodic
if there is a nonzero vector

{\boldsymbol{n}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus\{{\boldsymbol{0}}\}
such that
x=\sigma^{\boldsymbol{n}}(x)
,
and otherwise it is
nonperiodic
.
We say that a nonempty subshift
X
is
aperiodic

if the shift action
\sigma
on
X
is free.

For any subset
S\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
let
\pi_{S}:\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\to\mathcal{A}^{S}
denote the
projection map which restricts every
x\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
to
S
.
A
pattern
is a function
p\in\mathcal{A}^{S}
for some finite subset

S\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
To every pattern
p\in\mathcal{A}^{S}
corresponds
a subset
\pi_{S}^{-1}(p)\subset\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
called
cylinder
.
A nonempty set
X\subset\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is a

subshift
if and only if there exists a set
\mathcal{F}

of
forbidden
patterns such that

X=\{x\in\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\mid\pi_{S}\circ\sigma^{\boldsymbol{n}}(x)\notin\mathcal{F}\text{ for every }{\boldsymbol{n}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\text{ and }S\subset\mathbb{Z}^{d}\},

(2.2)

see
[
Hoc16
, Prop. 9.2.4]
.
A subshift
X\subset\mathcal{A}^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
is a

subshift of finite type
(SFT) if there exists a finite set
\mathcal{F}
such that (
2.2
) holds.
In this article, we consider subshifts of finite type on
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
, that is, the case

d=2
.

2.3
Wang tiles

A
Wang tile

is a tuple of four colors
(a,b,c,d)\in I\times J\times I\times J

where
I

is a finite set of vertical colors
and
J

is a finite set of horizontal colors; see

[
Wan61
,
Rob71
]
.
A Wang tile is represented as a unit square with colored edges:

a
b
c
d

For each Wang tile
\tau=(a,b,c,d)
, let

\textsc{East}(\tau)=a
,

\textsc{North}(\tau)=b
,

\textsc{West}(\tau)=c
,

\textsc{South}(\tau)=d

denote respectively the
colors
or
labels
of the right, top, left
and bottom edges of the tile
\tau
.

Let
\mathcal{T}=\{t_{0},\dots,t_{m-1}\}
be a set of Wang tiles.
A configuration
x:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\{0,\dots,m-1\}
is
valid
with respect to
\mathcal{T}
if
it assigns a tile in
\mathcal{T}
to each position of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, so that contiguous edges
of adjacent tiles have the same color, that is,

\displaystyle\textsc{East}(t_{x({\boldsymbol{n}})})

\displaystyle=\textsc{West}(t_{x({\boldsymbol{n}}+{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1})})

(2.3)

\displaystyle\textsc{North}(t_{x({\boldsymbol{n}})})

\displaystyle=\textsc{South}(t_{x({\boldsymbol{n}}+{\boldsymbol{e}}_{2})})

(2.4)

for every
{\boldsymbol{n}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
where
{\boldsymbol{e}}_{1}=(1,0)
and
{\boldsymbol{e}}_{2}=(0,1)
.

Let
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}=\{x\in\{0,\dots,m-1\}^{\mathbb{Z}^{2}}|x\text{ is a valid configuration with respect to }\mathcal{T}\}
.
Together with the shift action
\sigma
of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
the set
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
is a subshift which we called the
Wang shift
of
\mathcal{T}
.
The subshift
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
is of finite type satisfying (
2.2
)
since there exists a finite set of
forbidden patterns made of all horizontal and vertical dominoes of two tiles
whose adjacent edge is not of the same color.
To a configuration
x\in\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
corresponds a tiling of the plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by
the tiles
\mathcal{T}
where the unit square Wang tile
t_{x({\boldsymbol{n}})}
is placed at position
{\boldsymbol{n}}
for every

{\boldsymbol{n}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

A configuration
x\in\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
is
periodic
if there exists

{\boldsymbol{n}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
such that
x=\sigma^{\boldsymbol{n}}(x)
.
A set
\mathcal{T}
of Wang tiles is
periodic
if there exists a periodic configuration

x\in\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
.
Originally, Wang thought that every set
\mathcal{T}
of Wang tiles is periodic
as soon as
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
is nonempty
[
Wan61
]
.
Wang noticed that if this statement were true,
it would imply the existence of an algorithm
solving the
domino problem
, that is, taking as input a set of Wang tiles
and returning
yes
or
no
whether there exists a valid
configuration with these tiles.
Berger, a student of Wang, later proved that the domino problem is undecidable
and he also provided a first example of an aperiodic set of Wang tiles

[
Ber66
]
.
A set
\mathcal{T}
of Wang tiles is
aperiodic
if
the Wang shift
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
is a nonempty aperiodic subshift.

2.4
Wang tiles with stripes

We say that a set of Wang tiles

\mathcal{T}\subset I\times J\times I\times J

has horizontal stripes

if there exists a partition
I=I_{0}\cup I_{1}

of the vertical labels such that

\mathcal{T}\subset\left(I_{0}\times J\times I_{0}\times J\right)\cup\left(I_{1}\times J\times I_{1}\times J\right).

We say that a set of Wang tiles

\mathcal{T}\subset I\times J\times I\times J

has vertical stripes

if there exists a partition

J=J_{0}\cup J_{1}

of the horizontal labels
such that

\mathcal{T}\subset\left(I\times J_{0}\times I\times J_{0}\right)\cup\left(I\times J_{1}\times I\times J_{1}\right).

When drawing the Wang tiles,
we associate the subset
I_{1}
with horizontal stripes
and the subset
J_{1}
with vertical stripes,
whereas the subsets
I_{0}
and
J_{0}
are drawn with no stripe.
If a set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}
has horizontal and vertical stripes
with partitions
I=I_{0}\cup I_{1}
and
J=J_{0}\cup J_{1}
,
then we define the following subsets

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\square}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}\cap\left(I_{0}\times J_{0}\times I_{0}\times J_{0}\right),

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}\cap\left(I_{0}\times J_{1}\times I_{0}\times J_{1}\right),

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}\cap\left(I_{1}\times J_{0}\times I_{1}\times J_{0}\right),

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}\cap\left(I_{1}\times J_{1}\times I_{1}\times J_{1}\right).

which form a partition of

\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{\square}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
.

3
A sufficient condition for non-periodicity

In this article, we construct a relation between sets of Wang tiles
and planar Euclidean geometry. Under some condition, we can associate
a quadrilateral in the plane with a set of Wang tiles with stripes.
The relation is given precisely by the following definition
which is used in the statement of most of the results in this article.

Definition 3.1

(when a set of Wang tiles with stripes determines a quadrilateral)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}\subset I\times J\times I\times J
be a set of Wang tiles
having horizontal and vertical stripes with partition

\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{\square}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
.
Suppose the labels are encoded into vectors
using two maps
\textsc{v}:I\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\textsc{h}:J\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
If there exist
A,B,C,D\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that

\textsc{v}(a)+\textsc{h}(b)-\textsc{v}(c)-\textsc{h}(d)=\begin{cases}A&\text{ if }\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\square},\\
B&\text{ if }\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus},\\
C&\text{ if }\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar},\\
D&\text{ if }\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus},\end{cases}

(3.1)

for every tile

\tau=\hbox to25.13pt{\vbox to27.27pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.9444pt\lower-2.25284pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 31.5 L 31.5 31.5 L 31.5 0 Z M 31.5 31.5}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
{}{{}}{}
{}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 M 0 0 L 0 31.5 L 31.5 31.5 L 31.5 0 Z M 31.5 31.5}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{}{}{{

{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{15.56694pt}{9.22836pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.38861pt}{9.22836pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{8.7787pt}{1.08017pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\in\mathcal{T}
,
then we say that the set
\mathcal{T}

(and the functions
v
and
h
)

determines the quadrilateral
ABDC
.

Note that the quadrilateral can be degenerate as vertices may coincide or three
or more of them can be collinear.
When a set of Wang tiles with stripes determines a quadrilateral, then
we can show that the densities of vertical and horizontal stripes
must satisfy a system of equations related to the vertices of the quadrilateral.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{T}\subset I\times J\times I\times J
be a set of Wang tiles
having horizontal and vertical stripes with partition

\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{\square}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{T}
determines the quadrilateral
ABDC
for some
A,B,C,D\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
If there exists a doubly-periodic valid configuration
w:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathcal{T}
where
vertical stripes (
i.e.
, tiles in
\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
) have density
\alpha
, and the horizontal stripes (tiles in

\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
) have density
\beta
, then
the densities satisfy Equation (
1.1
):

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)=(1-\alpha)\left((1-\beta)A+\beta B\right)+\alpha\left((1-\beta)C+\beta D\right).

Proof.

Suppose that
there exists a valid configuration

w:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathcal{T}
over the set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}

which has two linearly independent periods.
Following the paragraph that follows Proposition 5.9 in
[
BG13
]
,
we may suppose that the periods are horizontal
and vertical and of the same length
K\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
.

Let
S_{K}=\{(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\colon 0\leq i,j<K\}
.
Since tiles in the same row either all have a horizontal stripe
or none of them have one (and similarly with columns and vertical stripes),
the density of tiles belonging to each subset of the partition

\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{\square}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}

is given by the following products:

\displaystyle\frac{\#\left(w^{-1}(\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus})\cap S_{K}\right)}{\#S_{K}}

\displaystyle=(1-\alpha)\beta,

\displaystyle\frac{\#\left(w^{-1}(\mathcal{T}_{\square})\cap S_{K}\right)}{\#S_{K}}

\displaystyle=(1-\alpha)(1-\beta),

\displaystyle\frac{\#\left(w^{-1}(\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus})\cap S_{K}\right)}{\#S_{K}}

\displaystyle=\alpha\beta,

\displaystyle\frac{\#\left(w^{-1}(\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar})\cap S_{K}\right)}{\#S_{K}}

\displaystyle=\alpha(1-\beta).

On the one hand, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\#S_{K}}\sum_{n\in S_{K}}\textsc{v}(\textsc{East}(w_{n}))+\textsc{h}(\textsc{North}(w_{n}))-\textsc{v}(\textsc{West}(w_{n}))-\textsc{h}(\textsc{South}(w_{n}))

(3.2)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\#S_{K}}\Big(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}\\
A_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\cdot\#\{n\in S_{K}\colon w_{n}\in\mathcal{T}_{\square}\}+\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}\\
B_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\cdot\#\{n\in S_{K}\colon w_{n}\in\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\}

\displaystyle+\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C_{1}\\
C_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\cdot\#\{n\in S_{K}\colon w_{n}\in\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\}+\left(\begin{smallmatrix}D_{1}\\
D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\cdot\#\{n\in S_{K}\colon w_{n}\in\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}\}\Big)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}\\
A_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)(1-\alpha)(1-\beta)+\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}\\
B_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)(1-\alpha)\beta+\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C_{1}\\
C_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\alpha(1-\beta)+\left(\begin{smallmatrix}D_{1}\\
D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\alpha\beta

\displaystyle=(1-\alpha)\left[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}\\
A_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)(1-\beta)+\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}\\
B_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\beta\right]+\alpha\left[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C_{1}\\
C_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)(1-\beta)+\left(\begin{smallmatrix}D_{1}\\
D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\beta\right].

On the other hand, since
w
is a valid configuration, the following sum is
telescoping and simplifies to zero because
K
is a horizontal and vertical
period of
w
:

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in S_{K}}\textsc{v}(\textsc{East}(w_{n}))+\textsc{h}(\textsc{North}(w_{n}))-\textsc{v}(\textsc{West}(w_{n}))-\textsc{h}(\textsc{South}(w_{n}))

(3.3)

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq i,j<K}\textsc{v}(\textsc{East}(w_{i,j}))-\textsc{v}(\textsc{West}(w_{i,j}))+\textsc{h}(\textsc{North}(w_{i,j}))-\textsc{h}(\textsc{South}(w_{i,j}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq i,j<K}\textsc{v}(\textsc{East}(w_{i,j}))-\textsc{v}(\textsc{East}(w_{i-1,j}))+\textsc{h}(\textsc{North}(w_{i,j}))-\textsc{h}(\textsc{North}(w_{i,j-1}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}\left(\textsc{East}(w_{i,j})-\textsc{East}(w_{i-1,j})\right)+\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\left(\textsc{North}(w_{i,j})-\textsc{North}(w_{i,j-1})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\left(\textsc{East}(w_{K-1,j})-\textsc{East}(w_{0-1,j})\right)+\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}\left(\textsc{North}(w_{i,K-1})-\textsc{North}(w_{i,0-1})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}0+\sum_{i=0}^{K-1}0=0.

Combining (
3.2
) and (
3.3
),
we obtain the equation given in (
1.1
).
∎

Under some conditions on determinants which are satisfied in many cases (in
fact all examples described in Section
4
satisfy these
conditions), Equation (
1.1
) has a solution which is easy
to describe.

Lemma 3.3
.

Suppose that
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{C}
is a solution
to Equation (
1.1
)
for some

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}\\
A_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}\\
B_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C_{1}\\
C_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}D_{1}\\
D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
If

\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}&D_{1}\\
A_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=0\quad\text{and}\quad\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}&D_{1}\\
B_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=-\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C_{1}&D_{1}\\
C_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\neq 0,

(3.4)

then

\alpha=\beta
and
\alpha
is a solution of the quadratic equation

\displaystyle 0=(A_{i}-B_{i}-C_{i}+D_{i})x^{2}+(B_{i}+C_{i}-2A_{i})x+A_{i}

(3.5)

whose discriminant is
\Delta=(B_{i}+C_{i})^{2}-4A_{i}D_{i}

for both
i=1
and
i=2
.

Proof.

Multiplying Equation (
1.1
) by
the matrix

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}D_{2}&0\\
0&D_{1}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

we obtain the following two equations:

\begin{cases}0=(1-\alpha)\left(D_{2}A_{1}(1-\beta)+D_{2}B_{1}\beta\right)+\alpha\left(D_{2}C_{1}(1-\beta)+D_{2}D_{1}\beta\right),\\
0=(1-\alpha)\left(D_{1}A_{2}(1-\beta)+D_{1}B_{2}\beta\right)+\alpha\left(D_{1}C_{2}(1-\beta)+D_{1}D_{2}\beta\right).\end{cases}

Since

\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}&D_{1}\\
A_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=0
,
the difference of the two equations yields

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=(D_{2}B_{1}-D_{1}B_{2})(1-\alpha)\beta+(D_{2}C_{1}-D_{1}C_{2})\alpha(1-\beta)

\displaystyle=\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}&D_{1}\\
B_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)(1-\alpha)\beta+\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C_{1}&D_{1}\\
C_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\alpha(1-\beta)

\displaystyle=\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}&D_{1}\\
B_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\left(\beta-\alpha\beta-\alpha-\alpha\beta\right)=\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}&D_{1}\\
B_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)(\beta-\alpha)

Since
\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}&D_{1}\\
B_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\neq 0
,
we must have equality of the densities, that is
\alpha=\beta
, and

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=A_{i}(1-\alpha)^{2}+(B_{i}+C_{i})(1-\alpha)\alpha+D_{i}\alpha^{2},

\displaystyle=(A_{i}-B_{i}-C_{i}+D_{i})\alpha^{2}+(B_{i}+C_{i}-2A_{i})\alpha+A_{i}

whose discriminant is
\Delta=(B_{i}+C_{i})^{2}-4A_{i}D_{i}

for both
i=1
and
i=2
.
∎

The solutions to Equation (
1.1
)
can be interpreted geometrically as the lines passing through the origin and
tangent to a parabola (see Figure
2
).
The complete description of the solutions for all cases
is postponed to Section
6
.
However, Lemma
3.3

is already sufficient to conclude nonperiodicity of many sets of Wang tiles.

Theorem
A
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a set of Wang tiles
having horizontal and vertical stripes with partition

\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{\square}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{T}
determines the quadrilateral
ABDC
for some integer points

A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}\\
A_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),B=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}\\
B_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),C=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C_{1}\\
C_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),D=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}D_{1}\\
D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
If

\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}&D_{1}\\
A_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=0,\qquad\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}&D_{1}\\
B_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=-\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C_{1}&D_{1}\\
C_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\neq 0

and there exists
i\in\{1,2\}
such that
(B_{i}+C_{i})^{2}-4A_{i}D_{i}>0
is not a perfect square,
then the set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}
admits no periodic tiling.

Proof.

By contradiction, assume there exists a
valid configuration
w:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathcal{T}
over the set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}

which is periodic.
From a standard argument
[
BG13
, Proposition 5.9]
,
there exists another valid configuration

w^{\prime}:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathcal{T}
over the set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}

which has two linearly independent periods.
In particular, the densities of all tiles in the configuration
w^{\prime}
exist,
as well as the density
\alpha
of tiles with vertical stripes (tiles in

\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
)
and the density
\beta
of tiles with horizontal stripes (tiles in

\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
) in
w^{\prime}
.
Since
w^{\prime}
is doubly-periodic, these densities must be rational numbers.
Thus,
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{Q}
.

From Lemma
3.2
,
the densities
\alpha
and
\beta
satisfy
Equation (
1.1
).
From Lemma
3.3
,
we have
\alpha=\beta
and

\alpha
and
\beta

also satisfy the quadratic Equation (
3.5
)
for both
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Since there exists
i\in\{1,2\}
such that

(B_{i}+C_{i})^{2}-4A_{i}D_{i}>0
is not a perfect square,
then at least one of the two equations is of degree 2
(because equality
A_{i}+D_{i}=B_{i}+C_{i}
implies that the discriminant is

(A_{i}-D_{i})^{2}
which is a perfect square)
and has no rational solution.
In particular,
\alpha=\beta\notin\mathbb{Q}
.
This is a contradiction and we conclude that
the set of Wang tiles
\mathcal{T}
admits no periodic tiling.
∎

Corollary 3.4
.

The set
\mathcal{T}_{Ammann}

of 16 Wang tiles admits no periodic tiling.

Proof.

As observed in the introduction,
the Ammann set of Wang tiles determines the quadrilateral
ABDC
with

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}&B_{1}&C_{1}&D_{1}\\
A_{2}&B_{2}&C_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0&1&-1\\
0&1&-1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)

satisfying

\displaystyle\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}&D_{1}\\
A_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&-1\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)=0,

\displaystyle\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}&D_{1}\\
B_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)=1\neq 0,

\displaystyle\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C_{1}&D_{1}\\
C_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&-1\\
-1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)=-1=-\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}&D_{1}\\
B_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right).

The discriminant

\Delta=(B_{1}+C_{1})^{2}-4A_{1}D_{1}=(0+1)^{2}+4=5

is positive and is not a perfect square.
Thus, we conclude from Theorem
A
that
every valid configuration over these Wang tiles is non-periodic.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

Let

A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}\\
A_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,

B=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}\\
B_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,

C=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C_{1}\\
C_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
and

D=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}D_{1}\\
D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
The hypothesis on the determinants in the statement of the theorem
means that
A
,
D
and
O=(0,0)
are collinear
and that the points
B
and
C
are equidistant to the line
AOD

but on two different side of that line.
Also, the quadrilateral
OBDC
has area

\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}&D_{1}\\
B_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=-\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C_{1}&D_{1}\\
C_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\neq 0
.

Observe that the Theorem holds but is inconclusive if we use the trivial partition

I_{0}=J_{0}=\varnothing
or the trivial partition
I_{1}=J_{1}=\varnothing
.
In these cases, the hypotheses of the theorem are not satisfied, since

I_{0}=J_{0}=\varnothing
implies
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
implies
\alpha=\beta=1
implies
D_{1}=D_{2}=0
,
while

I_{1}=J_{1}=\varnothing
implies
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{\square}
implies
\alpha=\beta=0
implies
A_{1}=A_{2}=0
.
The discriminant is a perfect square in these cases.

In the section that follows,
we apply Theorem
A

to conclude nonperiodicity of many well-known sets of Wang tiles.

4
Applications: new proofs of non-periodicity of known sets of Wang tiles

Theorem
A
may be used to prove the non-existence of
periodic tilings for many other well-known sets of Wang tiles.
We apply this method to
the encoding of the Penrose tilings into 24 Wang tiles
and to the family of metallic mean Wang tiles.
First, we present an encoding of Wang tiles as matrices which we use thereafter.

4.1
Wang tiles encoded as matrices

Let
\mathcal{T}\subset I\times J\times I\times J
be a set of Wang tiles
having horizontal and vertical stripes
with partitions
I=I_{0}\cup I_{1}
and
J=J_{0}\cup J_{1}

and

\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{\square}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
.
Suppose the labels are encoded into vectors
using two maps
v
and
h
:

\textsc{v}:I\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\textsc{h}:J\to\mathbb{R}^{n}.

Let
\mathbb{I}_{A}
denote the indicator function of a set
A
defined as

\mathbb{I}_{A}(x)=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if }x\in A,\\
0&\text{ if }x\notin A.\end{cases}

In this work,
we encode a Wang tile
\tau=(a,b,c,d)

as a
(n+1)\times 4
matrix whose
first row encodes the presence of stripes (vertical label in
I_{1}
or
horizontal label in
J_{1}
) and whose columns of the remaining part are the
images of its labels under the maps
v
and
h
:

M_{\tau}:=M^{\textsc{v},\textsc{h}}(\tau)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c}\mathbb{I}_{I_{1}}(a)&\mathbb{I}_{J_{1}}(b)&\mathbb{I}_{I_{1}}(c)&\mathbb{I}_{J_{1}}(d)\\
&&&\\[-8.53581pt]
\hline\cr&&&\\[-8.53581pt]
\textsc{v}(a)&\textsc{h}(b)&\textsc{v}(c)&\textsc{h}(d)\\
\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c}a_{0}&b_{0}&c_{0}&d_{0}\\
a_{1}&b_{1}&c_{1}&d_{1}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
a_{n}&b_{n}&c_{n}&d_{n}\end{array}\right).

If
e_{1}=(1,0,\dots,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, the partition

\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{\square}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}

is equivalently defined by the first row of the matrices:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\square}

\displaystyle=\{\tau\in\mathcal{T}\colon e_{1}M_{\tau}=(0,0,0,0)\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}

\displaystyle=\{\tau\in\mathcal{T}\colon e_{1}M_{\tau}=(0,1,0,1)\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}

\displaystyle=\{\tau\in\mathcal{T}\colon e_{1}M_{\tau}=(1,0,1,0)\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}

\displaystyle=\{\tau\in\mathcal{T}\colon e_{1}M_{\tau}=(1,1,1,1)\}.

Now, suppose that
n=2
and let
U=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
In this situation, the last two lines of the matrix
M_{\tau}

are the image of the labels under the maps
v
and
h
:

U\cdot M_{\tau}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c}\textsc{v}(a)&\textsc{h}(b)&\textsc{v}(c)&\textsc{h}(d)\\
\end{array}\right).

Therefore,
the left hand side of Equation (
3.1
)
is equal to

U\cdot M_{\tau}\cdot\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
1\\
-1\\
-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\textsc{v}(a)+\textsc{h}(b)-\textsc{v}(c)-\textsc{h}(d).

(4.1)

We use this matrix notation of Wang tiles in
this section in the proof of
Theorem
4.2
,
Theorem
4.3

and later in the proof of
Proposition
7.1
.

Validity of configurations translates into the following relation on the matrix
encoding of Wang tiles.

Remark 4.1
.

If
w:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathcal{T}
is a valid configuration over the tiles
\mathcal{T}
,
then

M^{\textsc{v},\textsc{h}}(w_{n})\left(\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
0\\
0\\
0\end{array}\right)=M^{\textsc{v},\textsc{h}}(w_{n+e_{1}})\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
0\\
1\\
0\end{array}\right)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad M^{\textsc{v},\textsc{h}}(w_{n})\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
1\\
0\\
0\end{array}\right)=M^{\textsc{v},\textsc{h}}(w_{n+e_{2}})\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
0\\
0\\
1\end{array}\right)

for every
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}

where
\{e_{1},e_{2}\}
denotes the canonical base of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

4.2
Penrose encoded into 24 Wang tiles

As observed in
[
GS87
, Exercise 11.1.2]
,
Penrose tilings can be encoded into a set
\mathcal{T}_{24}
of 24 Wang tiles.
This was studied in more details in

[
Jan21
,
JR25
]
.
Here, we use the labelling of the Wang tiles proposed in this blog post
1
1

1

http://www.slabbe.org/blogue/2025/05/on-the-bifurcation-diagram-proposed-by-jang-and-robinson/
.

Using the sufficient condition in Theorem
A
,
we may prove that the set
\mathcal{T}_{24}
admits no periodic tiling of the plane.

Theorem 4.2
.

The set
\mathcal{T}_{24}

of 24 Wang tiles encoding Penrose tilings
admits no periodic tiling.

Proof.

We map the vertical and horizontal labels to vectors as follows:

\textsc{v}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}A\mapsto(1,3,1),&F\mapsto(0,1,1),\\
B\mapsto(1,3,2),&G\mapsto(1,4,2),\\
C\mapsto(1,0,0),&H\mapsto(1,2,1),\\
D\mapsto(1,6,3),&I\mapsto(0,0,0),\\
E\mapsto(0,3,2),&J\mapsto(0,4,3),\end{array}\right.\quad\text{ and }\quad\textsc{h}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}A\mapsto(1,2,2),&F\mapsto(0,1,2),\\
B\mapsto(1,0,1),&G\mapsto(1,1,1),\\
C\mapsto(1,1,3),&H\mapsto(1,1,2),\\
D\mapsto(1,1,0),&I\mapsto(0,2,3),\\
E\mapsto(0,1,1),&J\mapsto(0,0,0).\end{array}\right.

Using the vector
e=(1,0,0)
, the partition of the set of Wang tiles is:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\square}

\displaystyle=\{2,3\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}

\displaystyle=\{10,11,12,13,20,21\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}

\displaystyle=\{8,9,14,15,22,23\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}

\displaystyle=\{0,1,4,5,6,7,16,17,18,19\}.

Now take
U=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
For each of the Wang tiles, we have

•

if
\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\square}
, then

\displaystyle M_{\tau}

\displaystyle\in\{M_{2},M_{3}\}=\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0&0&0\\
3&1&0&2\\
2&1&0&3\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0&0&0\\
4&0&1&1\\
3&0&1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right\}

which all satisfy
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)M_{\tau}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
1\\
-1\\
-1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,

•

if
\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}
, then

\displaystyle M_{\tau}

\displaystyle\in\{M_{10},M_{11},M_{12},M_{13},M_{20},M_{21}\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0&1&0\\
4&1&0&1\\
2&2&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0&1&0\\
6&1&2&1\\
3&2&1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0&1&0\\
3&2&0&1\\
2&3&0&2\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0&1&0\\
6&1&3&0\\
3&1&1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0&1&0\\
6&1&3&0\\
3&2&2&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0&1&0\\
3&2&0&1\\
1&3&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right\}

which all satisfy
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)M_{\tau}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
1\\
-1\\
-1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}4\\
3\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,

•

if
\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}
, then

\displaystyle M_{\tau}

\displaystyle\in\{M_{8},M_{9},M_{14},M_{15},M_{22},M_{23}\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&0&1\\
1&1&3&1\\
1&1&2&3\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&0&1\\
1&1&3&1\\
1&0&2&2\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&0&1\\
0&0&1&1\\
0&1&1&3\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&0&1\\
3&1&4&2\\
2&0&3&2\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&0&1\\
1&1&4&0\\
1&0&3&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&0&1\\
0&2&3&1\\
0&2&2&3\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right\}

which all satisfy
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)M_{\tau}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
1\\
-1\\
-1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-2\\
-3\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,

•

if
\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
, then

\displaystyle M_{\tau}

\displaystyle\in\{M_{0},M_{1},M_{4},M_{5},M_{6},M_{7},M_{16},M_{17},M_{18},M_{19}\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1&1&1\\
3&2&6&1\\
1&2&3&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1&1&1\\
0&1&3&0\\
0&3&2&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1&1&1\\
0&1&2&1\\
0&3&1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1&1&1\\
4&1&6&1\\
2&1&3&0\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1&1&1\\
2&1&3&2\\
1&2&1&2\end{smallmatrix}\right),\\[8.53581pt]
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1&1&1\\
3&0&4&1\\
2&1&2&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1&1&1\\
2&1&3&2\\
1&3&2&2\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1&1&1\\
0&1&3&0\\
0&2&1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1&1&1\\
3&2&6&1\\
2&2&3&1\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1&1&1\\
3&0&4&1\\
1&1&2&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\end{array}\right\}

which all satisfy
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)M_{\tau}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
1\\
-1\\
-1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-2\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

Thus, we have that the set of Wang tiles determines the quadrilateral
ABDC

with

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}&B_{1}&C_{1}&D_{1}\\
A_{2}&B_{2}&C_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&4&-2&-2\\
0&3&-3&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)

satisfying

\displaystyle\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}&D_{1}\\
A_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2&-2\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)=0,

\displaystyle\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}&D_{1}\\
B_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}4&-2\\
3&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)=6\neq 0,

\displaystyle\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C_{1}&D_{1}\\
C_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-2&-2\\
-3&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)=-6=-\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}&D_{1}\\
B_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right).

The discriminant

\Delta=(B_{1}+C_{1})^{2}-4A_{1}D_{1}=(4-2)^{2}+16=20

is positive and is not a perfect square.
Thus, we conclude from Theorem
A
that
every valid configuration over these Wang tiles is non-periodic.
∎

4.3
Metallic mean Wang tiles

A family of aperiodic set of Wang tiles was introduced in
[
Lab25a
,
Lab25b
]

associated with metallic mean numbers.
Although it was proved in
[
Lab25b
]
that tilings by metallic mean Wang tiles
are associated with irrational rotations on a torus, the proof of non-periodicity
of these tilings was deduced from its self-similarity and its minimality
proved in
[
Lab25a
]
. A less tedious proof of non-periodicity of these tilings
based on a short Kari-like argument
[
Kar96
]
was left open.
In this section, using Theorem
A
, we obtain
a short proof of non-periodicity of every tiling of the plane by metallic mean Wang tiles.

We recall from
[
Lab25b
]
the definition of the metallic mean Wang tiles
as instances of a computer chip.
For every integer
n\geq 1
, let
V_{n}\subset\mathbb{N}^{3}
be a finite subset of vectors

V_{n}=\{(v_{0},v_{1},v_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{3}\colon 0\leq v_{0}\leq v_{1}\leq 1\text{ and }v_{1}\leq v_{2}\leq n+1\}

with nondecreasing entries where the middle entry is at most 1.
For every integer
n\geq 1
, a function

\begin{array}[]{rccl}\theta_{n}:&V_{n}\times V_{n}&\to&\mathbb{Z}^{3}\\
&(u_{0},u_{1},u_{2}),(v_{0},v_{1},v_{2})&\mapsto&(r_{0},r_{1},r_{2}),\end{array}

taking two vectors as input and returning one vector
is defined by the rule

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}&r_{0}=u_{0},\\
&r_{1}=\begin{cases}v_{2}-n&\text{ if }u_{0}=0,\\
1&\text{ if }u_{0}=1,\end{cases}\\
&r_{2}=\begin{cases}v_{1}+u_{0}&\text{ if }v_{0}=0,\\
u_{2}+1&\text{ if }v_{0}=1.\end{cases}\end{array}\right.

(4.2)

The set of metallic mean Wang tiles can be defined as the set of instances of a
square-shape computer chip outputing the value of map
\theta_{n}
on the right
and on the top depending on the left input
u
and right input
v

[
Lab25b
]
.
More precisely, it is the set

\mathcal{C}_{n}=\left\{\raisebox{-27.0301pt}{
\hbox to141.83pt{\vbox to62.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 73.22255pt\lower-16.86601pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-17.91475pt}{-11.033pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\middle|\,u,v\in V_{n}\text{ such that }\theta_{n}(u,v),\theta_{n}(v,u)\in V_{n}\right\}

(4.3)

which is the finite set of all possible instances of the
\theta_{n}
-chip.

Theorem 4.3
.

For every
n\geq 1
, the set
\mathcal{C}_{n}
of metallic mean Wang tiles
admits no periodic tiling.

Proof.

We use the description of the metallic mean Wang tiles as instances of a
computer chip recalled in (
4.3
).
But, when encoding the Wang tiles into matrices, we use the vectors in

V_{n}
for the vertical labels:

\textsc{v}:(u_{0},u_{1},u_{2})\mapsto(1-u_{0},u_{1},u_{2}),

and we swap the last two entries of the vectors in
V_{n}
for the horizontal labels:

\textsc{h}:(v_{0},v_{1},v_{2})\mapsto(1-v_{0},v_{2},v_{1}).

Thus, we have the following matrix representation of the Wang tiles

\displaystyle M_{(\theta_{n}(u,v),\theta_{n}(v,u),u,v)}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c}\textsc{v}(\theta_{n}(u,v))&\textsc{h}(\theta_{n}(v,u))&\textsc{v}(u)&\textsc{h}(v)\\
\end{array}\right).

Now take
U=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
Depending on the value of
u_{0},v_{0}\in\{0,1\}
, we have the following four subsets
of Wang tiles:

•

if
\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\square}
, then

M_{\tau}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}1-u_{0}&1-v_{0}&1-u_{0}&1-v_{0}\\
1&v_{2}+1&u_{1}&v_{2}\\
u_{2}+1&1&u_{2}&v_{1}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}0&0&0&0\\
1&v_{2}+1&1&v_{2}\\
u_{2}+1&1&u_{2}&1\end{array}\right)

which satisfy

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)M_{\tau}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
1\\
-1\\
-1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

•

if
\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}
, then

M_{\tau}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}1-u_{0}&1-v_{0}&1-u_{0}&1-v_{0}\\
1&v_{2}+1&u_{1}&v_{2}\\
v_{1}+u_{0}&u_{2}-n&u_{2}&v_{1}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}0&1&0&1\\
1&v_{2}+1&1&v_{2}\\
v_{1}+1&u_{2}-n&u_{2}&v_{1}\end{array}\right)

which satisfy

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)M_{\tau}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
1\\
-1\\
-1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
1-n\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,

•

if
\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}
, then

M_{\tau}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}1-u_{0}&1-v_{0}&1-u_{0}&1-v_{0}\\
v_{2}-n&u_{1}+v_{0}&u_{1}&v_{2}\\
u_{2}+1&1&u_{2}&v_{1}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}1&0&1&0\\
v_{2}-n&u_{1}+1&u_{1}&v_{2}\\
u_{2}+1&1&u_{2}&1\end{array}\right)

which satisfy

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)M_{\tau}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
1\\
-1\\
-1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1-n\\
1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,

•

if
\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
, then

M_{\tau}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}1-u_{0}&1-v_{0}&1-u_{0}&1-v_{0}\\
v_{2}-n&u_{1}+v_{0}&u_{1}&v_{2}\\
v_{1}+u_{0}&u_{2}-n&u_{2}&v_{1}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}1&1&1&1\\
v_{2}-n&u_{1}&u_{1}&v_{2}\\
v_{1}&u_{2}-n&u_{2}&v_{1}\end{array}\right)

which satisfy

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)M_{\tau}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
1\\
-1\\
-1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-n\\
-n\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,

Thus, we have that the set of Wang tiles determines the quadrilateral
ABDC
where

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}&B_{1}&C_{1}&D_{1}\\
A_{2}&B_{2}&C_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1&1-n&-n\\
1&1-n&1&-n\end{smallmatrix}\right)

satisfies

\displaystyle\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}&D_{1}\\
A_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&-n\\
1&-n\end{smallmatrix}\right)=0,

\displaystyle\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}&D_{1}\\
B_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&-n\\
1-n&-n\end{smallmatrix}\right)=-n+n(1-n)=-n^{2}\neq 0,

\displaystyle\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C_{1}&D_{1}\\
C_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1-n&-n\\
1&-n\end{smallmatrix}\right)=n^{2}=-\det\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}&D_{1}\\
B_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

since
n\geq 1
is a positive integer.
The discriminant

\Delta=(B_{1}+C_{1})^{2}-4A_{1}D_{1}=n^{2}+4

is positive and is not a perfect square when
n\geq 1
.
Thus, we conclude from Theorem
A
that
every valid configuration over these metallic mean Wang tiles is non-periodic.
∎

It is interesting to observe that the maps
v
and
h
used in the proof of
Theorem
4.3
almost correspond to the
labels of the metallic mean Wang tiles as 3-dimensional vectors.
This is all that was missing in order to provide a positive answer
to Question 8.5 in
[
Lab25b
]
.

5
Finding the quadrilateral determined by a set of Wang tiles with stripes

In this section, we present a method to compute
the maps
v
,
h
and the quadrilateral
ABDC
for a set
of Wang tiles with horizontal and vertical stripes.
This method was used for determining the maps
v
and
h

and the quadrilaterals for Ammann and Penrose examples in the previous section.
The strategy is based on linear algebra.

5.1
The pullback of two linear transformations

Let
U_{1}
,
U_{2}
and
V
be vector spaces
and
\lambda_{1}:U_{1}\to V
and
\lambda_{2}:U_{2}\to V

be two linear transformations.
Together with the natural projections

\pi_{1}:U_{1}\times U_{2}\to U_{1}

and

\pi_{2}:U_{1}\times U_{2}\to U_{2}
,
we have the following diagram

\hbox to108.44pt{\vbox to51.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 54.21727pt\lower-24.65279pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
