) illustrating
some typical examples.

We finish this section by observing that the quadrilateral
ABDC
circumscribed
to a parabola reminds one of Poncelet’s closure theorem
[
BKOR87
]
.
In geometry, this theorem states that whenever a polygon is inscribed in one conic
section and circumscribes another one, the polygon must
be part of an infinite family of polygons that are all inscribed in and
circumscribe the same two conics.
The special case of quadrilaterals, circles and parabolas is treated in more details in

[
DM25
]
.

6.3
Every pair of quadratic number field elements is a solution

One can prove more generally that if
\alpha,\beta
are two elements of the
same quadratic number field, then there exist
A,B,C,D\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that

(\alpha,\beta)
is a solution to Equation (
1.1
).

Lemma 6.3
.

For every pair
(\alpha,\beta)
of elements of a quadratic number field
K
,
there exist four non-collinear points
A,B,C,D\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that

(\alpha,\beta)
is a solution to Equation (
1.1
).

We can choose
A,B,C,D
so that opposite sides of
ABDC
are not parallel
if and only if
\alpha
and
\beta
are both irrational or both rational.

Proof.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in K
for some quadratic number field
K=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{d})

for some square-free integer
d
different from
0
and
1
.
Let

\xi=((1-\alpha)(1-\beta),(1-\alpha)\beta,\alpha(1-\beta),\alpha\beta)^{T}\in K^{4}

satisfying
(1,1,1,1)\cdot\xi=1
.
Since
\{1,\sqrt{d}\}
is a basis for
K
considered as a rational vector space,
there exists
V\in\mathbb{Q}^{4\times 2}
such that
\xi=V\cdot(1,\sqrt{d})^{T}
.
The dimension of
\text{ColumnSpace}(V)^{\perp}
is at least
4-2=2
.
Thus, let
M=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}&B_{1}&C_{1}&D_{1}\\
A_{2}&B_{2}&C_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2\times 4}

be any matrix whose rows are two linearly independent integer vectors
in
\text{ColumnSpace}(V)^{\perp}
.
The matrix
M
has rank 2 and its row space is orthogonal to the column space of
V
, that is,

MV=\mathbf{0}\in\mathbb{Q}^{2\times 2}
. Therefore,
Equation (
1.1
) is satisfied:

\displaystyle(1-\alpha)\left[(1-\beta)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}\\
A_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)+\beta\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}\\
B_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right]+\alpha\left[(1-\beta)\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C_{1}\\
C_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)+\beta\left(\begin{smallmatrix}D_{1}\\
D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=M\xi=M\cdot V\cdot\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
\sqrt{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\mathbf{0}\cdot\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
\sqrt{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right).

Suppose the columns
A,B,C,D
of
M
are collinear.
Then the span of
\text{RowSpace}(M)\cup\{(1,1,1,1)\}
has dimension less than 3, and since
\dim_{\mathbb{Q}}\text{RowSpace}(M)=2
,
we have
(1,1,1,1)\in\text{RowSpace}(M)
.
Thus, there exists
u\in\mathbb{Z}^{2\times 1}
such that
u\cdot M=(1,1,1,1)
.
However,

1=(1,1,1,1)\cdot\xi=uM\cdot V\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
\sqrt{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=u\cdot\mathbf{0}\cdot\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
\sqrt{d}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=0

a contradiction. We conclude that the columns of
M
are non-collinear.

Now suppose that
\alpha
and
\beta
are both irrational. Since the columns of
M
are non-collinear, cases (i) and (ii) of Proposition
6.2
do not hold. Since neither
\alpha
nor
\beta
is rational, we can exclude cases (iii) - (v), so (vi) must hold.

If both
\alpha
and
\beta
are rational, the first column of
V
is
\xi
, and the second is zero. Thus the rows of
M
are only required to be orthogonal to a single vector,
\xi
. Let
M\in\mathbb{Z}^{2\times 4}
be arbitrary, and set
M^{\prime}=M-M\cdot\xi\cdot(1,1,1,1)
.
Then

\displaystyle M^{\prime}\cdot\xi

\displaystyle=(M-M\cdot\xi\cdot(1,1,1,1))\cdot\xi=M\cdot\xi-M\cdot\xi\cdot(1,1,1,1)\cdot\xi=M\cdot\xi-M\cdot\xi\cdot 1=\mathbf{0}.

Since the columns of
M^{\prime}
are obtained by translating the columns of
M
by
M\cdot\xi\in\mathbb{Q}^{2}
, the shape of the quadrilateral determined by the columns is unchanged, so we can certainly ensure that opposite sides are not parallel. By multiplying
M^{\prime}
by an integer, we can obtain a matrix in
\mathbb{Z}^{2\times 4}
whose columns determine
A,B,C,D
.

Conversely, suppose exactly one of
\alpha
and
\beta
is rational; without loss of generality, we may assume it is
\beta
. From Proposition
6.2
and its proof, (iv) in the proposition holds, with the lines through
AB
and
CD
passing through the origin. Thus the quadrilateral
ABDC
has a pair of parallel opposite sides.
∎

7
Stripe densities always exist and satisfy Equation (
1.1
)

In this section, we prove a stronger result than Theorem
A
.
Under the same hypothesis, we show that densities of stripes in valid
configurations over a set of Wang tiles that determines the quadrilateral
ABDC

must exist and be a solution to Equation (
1.1
).

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
\mathcal{T}\subset I\times J\times I\times J
be a set of Wang tiles
having horizontal and vertical stripes with partition

\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{\square}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{T}
determines the quadrilateral
ABDC
for some

A,B,C,D\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
If Equation (
1.1
) has finitely many solutions,
then for every valid configuration
w:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathcal{T}
in
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
,
the density
\alpha
of tiles with vertical stripes (tiles in

\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
)
and
the density
\beta
of tiles with horizontal stripes (tiles in

\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
)
both exist, and
(\alpha,\beta)
is a solution to
Equation (
1.1
).

Proof.

Let
w:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathcal{T}
be a valid configuration over the set of tiles
\mathcal{T}
.
For every integer
K\geq 0
, we
consider the subdomain
S_{K}=\{(i,j)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\colon-K\leq i,j\leq K\}

of cardinality
(2K+1)^{2}
.
For every integer
K\geq 0
, let

\alpha_{K}=\frac{\#\left(w^{-1}(\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus})\cap S_{K}\right)}{(2K+1)^{2}}\quad\text{ and }\quad\beta_{K}=\frac{\#\left(w^{-1}(\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus})\cap S_{K}\right)}{(2K+1)^{2}}

be respectively
the density of vertical stripes
(tiles in
\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
)
and
the density of horizontal stripes
(tiles in
\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
)
in the configuration
w

restricted to the subdomain
S_{K}
.
Thus, in the domain
S_{K}
, the density of tiles in the configuration
w

belonging to each subset of the partition

\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{\square}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}

is given by the following formulas:

\displaystyle\frac{\#\left(w^{-1}(\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus})\cap S_{K}\right)}{\#S_{K}}

\displaystyle=(1-\alpha_{K})\beta_{K},

\displaystyle\frac{\#\left(w^{-1}(\mathcal{T}_{\square})\cap S_{K}\right)}{\#S_{K}}

\displaystyle=(1-\alpha_{K})(1-\beta_{K}),

\displaystyle\frac{\#\left(w^{-1}(\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus})\cap S_{K}\right)}{\#S_{K}}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{K}\beta_{K},

\displaystyle\frac{\#\left(w^{-1}(\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar})\cap S_{K}\right)}{\#S_{K}}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{K}(1-\beta_{K}).

In what follows, we identify
w_{n}
with its matrix encoding
M^{\textsc{v},\textsc{h}}(w_{n})

following Section
4.1
.
On the one hand, for every integer
K\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\#S_{K}}\sum_{n\in S_{K}}U\cdot w_{n}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
1\\
-1\\
-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\#S_{K}}\Big(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}\\
A_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\cdot\#\{n\in S_{K}\colon w_{n}\in\mathcal{T}_{\square}\}+\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}\\
B_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\cdot\#\{n\in S_{K}\colon w_{n}\in\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\}

(7.1)

\displaystyle+\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C_{1}\\
C_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\cdot\#\{n\in S_{K}\colon w_{n}\in\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\}+\left(\begin{smallmatrix}D_{1}\\
D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\cdot\#\{n\in S_{K}\colon w_{n}\in\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}\}\Big)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}\\
A_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)(1-\alpha_{K})(1-\beta_{K})+\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}\\
B_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)(1-\alpha_{K})\beta_{K}+\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C_{1}\\
C_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\alpha_{K}(1-\beta_{K})+\left(\begin{smallmatrix}D_{1}\\
D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\alpha_{K}\beta_{K}

\displaystyle=(1-\alpha_{K})\left[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}\\
A_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)(1-\beta_{K})+\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{1}\\
B_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\beta_{K}\right]+\alpha_{K}\left[\left(\begin{smallmatrix}C_{1}\\
C_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)(1-\beta_{K})+\left(\begin{smallmatrix}D_{1}\\
D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\beta_{K}\right].

On the other hand,
let
\{e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}\}
be the canonical base of
\mathbb{R}^{4}
.
Because
w
is a valid configuration,
Remark
4.1
applies
and the following sum is telescoping:

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in S_{K}}w_{n}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
1\\
-1\\
-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{-K\leq i,j\leq K}w_{i,j}e_{1}-w_{i,j}e_{3}+w_{i,j}e_{2}-w_{i,j}e_{4}

\displaystyle=\sum_{-K\leq i,j\leq K}w_{i,j}e_{1}-w_{i-1,j}e_{1}+w_{i,j}e_{2}-w_{i,j-1}e_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=-K}^{K}\sum_{i=-K}^{K}\left(w_{i,j}e_{1}-w_{i-1,j}e_{1}\right)+\sum_{i=-K}^{K}\sum_{j=-K}^{K}\left(w_{i,j}e_{2}-w_{i,j-1}e_{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=-K}^{K}\left(w_{K,j}e_{1}-w_{-K-1,j}e_{1}\right)+\sum_{i=-K}^{K}\left(w_{i,K}e_{2}-w_{i,-K-1}e_{2}\right).

Let
\rho=\max_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\|M_{\tau}\|
be the maximum norm of the
matrix encodings of the Wang tiles for the sup-norm

\|\cdot\|=\|\cdot\|_{\infty}

and let
\Delta=4\cdot\rho\cdot\|U\|
.
The following is bounded by a constant

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\#S_{K}}\left\|\sum_{n\in S_{K}}U\cdot w_{n}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
1\\
-1\\
-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right\|

(7.2)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|U\|}{(2K+1)^{2}}\left\|\sum_{j=-K}^{K}\left(w_{K,j}e_{1}-w_{-K-1,j}e_{1}\right)+\sum_{i=-K}^{K}\left(w_{i,K}e_{2}-w_{i,-K-1}e_{2}\right)\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|U\|}{(2K+1)^{2}}\left(\sum_{j=-K}^{K}\left(\|w_{K,j}e_{1}\|+\|w_{-K-1,j}e_{1}\|\right)+\sum_{i=-K}^{K}\left(\|w_{i,K}e_{2}\|+\|w_{i,-K-1}e_{2}\|\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|U\|}{2K+1}\left(\max_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\|M_{\tau}e_{1}\|+\max_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\|M_{\tau}e_{1}\|+\max_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\|M_{\tau}e_{2}\|+\max_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\|M_{\tau}e_{2}\|\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\|U\|}{2K+1}\left(2\max_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\|M_{\tau}e_{1}\|+2\max_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\|M_{\tau}e_{2}\|\right)\leq\frac{4\rho\|U\|}{2K+1}=\frac{\Delta}{2K+1}.

Combining (
7.1
) and (
7.2
),
and assuming the norm is the sup-norm,
we obtain the pair of estimates

\begin{cases}\left|(1-\alpha_{K})\left(A_{1}(1-\beta_{K})+B_{1}\beta_{K}\right)+\alpha_{K}\left(C_{1}(1-\beta_{K})+D_{1}\beta_{K}\right)\right|=\mathcal{O}(K^{-1}),\\
\left|(1-\alpha_{K})\left(A_{2}(1-\beta_{K})+B_{2}\beta_{K}\right)+\alpha_{K}\left(C_{2}(1-\beta_{K})+D_{2}\beta_{K}\right)\right|=\mathcal{O}(K^{-1}).\end{cases}

(7.3)

Define
P:[0,1]^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
by

\displaystyle P(\alpha,\beta)=

\displaystyle\left|(1-\alpha)\left(A_{1}(1-\beta)+B_{1}\beta\right)+\alpha\left(C_{1}(1-\beta)+D_{1}\beta\right)\right|

\displaystyle+\left|(1-\alpha)\left(A_{2}(1-\beta)+B_{2}\beta\right)+\alpha\left(C_{2}(1-\beta)+D_{2}\beta\right)\right|

Then
P
is continuous, and its set of zeros
\mathcal{Z}_{P}=P^{-1}(0)

is the set of solutions to Equation (
1.1
).
By hypothesis, Equation (
1.1
) has finitely many solutions
(\alpha,\beta)
.
If we set
x_{K}=(\alpha_{K},\beta_{K})\in[0,1]^{2}
for every integer
K\geq 0
,
then (
7.3
) implies
\lim_{K\to\infty}P(x_{K})=0
.

Since
\alpha_{K}=\frac{n_{K}}{2K+1}
, where
n_{K}
is the number of vertical stripes in
S_{K}
, and since
n_{K+1}=n_{K}+d_{K}
for some
d_{K}\in\{0,1,2\}
,

\alpha_{K+1}-\alpha_{K}=\frac{n_{K}+d_{K}}{2K+3}-\frac{n_{K}}{2K+1}=\frac{-2n_{K}+d_{K}(2K+1)}{(2K+1)(2K+3)}=\mathcal{O}(1/K).

The same estimate holds for
\beta_{K+1}-\beta_{K}
, so
\lim_{K\to\infty}\|x_{K+1}-x_{K}\|=0
.

Given
\epsilon>0
, let
X=[0,1]^{2}\smallsetminus\cup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}_{P}}B_{\epsilon}(z)
, and
\delta=\min_{y\in X}P(y),

which is positive, by continuity and compactness. Then

P(x_{K})<\delta\implies x_{K}\in[0,1]^{2}\smallsetminus X=\cup_{x\in\mathcal{Z}_{P}}B_{\epsilon}(x).

For
K
sufficiently large and
\epsilon
sufficiently small, we cannot have
x_{K}\in B_{\epsilon}(z_{1})
and
x_{K+1}\in B_{\epsilon}(z_{2})
for distinct
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathcal{Z}_{P}
, since
\mathcal{Z}_{P}
is finite. Thus for some
z_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}_{P}
, we have that for any
\epsilon>0
,
x_{K}\in B_{\epsilon}(z_{0})
for
K
sufficiently large, so
\lim_{K\to\infty}x_{K}=z_{0}
.
∎

Following Proposition
7.1
,
for each example in the Section
4
,
the stripe densities
(\alpha,\beta)

must satisfy
\alpha=\beta
and the equation

0=(A_{i}-B_{i}-C_{i}+D_{i})\alpha^{2}+(B_{i}+C_{i}-2A_{i})\alpha+A_{i}

for
i\in\{1,2\}
.
These equations and their solutions are listed in
Table
3
.

\begin{array}[]{l|c|c|c|c}\text{Example}&\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}&B_{1}&C_{1}&D_{1}\\
A_{2}&B_{2}&C_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)&\Delta&\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Equations}\\
\text{for }i\in\{1,2\}\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{c}\text{Stripe densities}\\
(\alpha,\beta)\end{array}\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr&&&\\[-5.69054pt]
\text{Penrose}&\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-2&2&0\\
1&1&0&-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)&5&\begin{array}[]{l}0=0\alpha^{2}+0\alpha+0\\
0=-\alpha^{2}-\alpha+1\end{array}&\alpha=\beta=\varphi^{-1}\\[8.53581pt]
\hline\cr&&&\\[-5.69054pt]
\text{Ammann}&\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&-1&0\\
1&1&0&-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)&5&\begin{array}[]{l}0=0\alpha^{2}+0\alpha+0\\
0=-\alpha^{2}-\alpha+1\end{array}&\alpha=\beta=\varphi^{-1}\\[8.53581pt]
\hline\cr&&&\\[-5.69054pt]
\text{19 self-similar $\mathcal{U}$}&\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-1&0&1&1\\
1&-2&1&-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)&5&\begin{array}[]{l}0=-\alpha^{2}+3\alpha-1\\
0=\alpha^{2}-3\alpha+1\end{array}&\alpha=\beta=\varphi^{-2}\\[8.53581pt]
\hline\cr&&&\\[-5.69054pt]
\text{16 self-similar $\mathcal{Z}$}&\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-1&-1&2&1\\
-1&2&-1&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)&5&\begin{array}[]{l}0=-\alpha^{2}+3\alpha-1\\
0=-\alpha^{2}+3\alpha-1\end{array}&\alpha=\beta=\varphi^{-2}\\[8.53581pt]
\hline\cr&&&\\[-5.69054pt]
\text{Jeandel-Rao $\mathcal{T}_{7}$}&\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0&-1&-1\\
1&1&-2&-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)&5&\begin{array}[]{l}0=\alpha^{2}-3\alpha+1\\
0=\alpha^{2}-3\alpha+1\end{array}&\alpha=\beta=\varphi^{-2}\\[8.53581pt]
\hline\cr&&&\\[-5.69054pt]
\text{Metallic mean}&\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1&1-n&-n\\
1&1-n&1&-n\end{smallmatrix}\right)&n^{2}+4&\begin{array}[]{l}0=-\alpha^{2}-n\alpha+1\\
0=-\alpha^{2}-n\alpha+1\end{array}&\alpha=\beta=\tau_{n}^{-1}\\[8.53581pt]
\hline\cr\end{array}

Table 3:

Densities
(\alpha,\beta)

of vertical and horizontal stripes
for every example in Section
4

where
\varphi
is the golden mean
and
\tau_{n}
is the
n
-th metallic mean.

8
New constructions of aperiodic sets of Wang tiles

In this section we design finite sets of Wang tiles with vertical and horizontal stripes that satisfy the linear constraints (
1.1
). Such tiles can be constructed for any integer parameter values
A_{i},B_{i},C_{i},D_{i}
as long as (
1.1
) admits a solution
\alpha,\beta\in[0,1]
. The constructions provide new families of aperiodic tile sets.

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
A,B,C,D\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
For every solution
\alpha,\beta\in[0,1]
to Equation (
1.1
),
there exists a finite Wang tile set

\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{\square}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}

with horizontal and vertical stripes such that

(i)

\mathcal{T}
admits a tiling
w:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathcal{T}
where vertical
stripes (
i.e.
, tiles in

\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
)
have density
\alpha
, and the horizontal stripes (tiles in
\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
) have density
\beta
, and

(ii)

\mathcal{T}
determines the quadrilateral
ABDC
.

Proof.

The colors of the tiles are triplets
(s,t,u)
of integers. The first coordinate
s
is
0
or
1
depending on whether there is a stripe in the tile across the edge, with label
1
indicating the presence of a stripe. The encoding functions
v
and
h
extract the last two coordinates of the vectors, i.e., they map
(s,t,u)\mapsto(t,u)
.

In the default tiling
w
, the second coordinate
t
of a horizontal edge is a digit in a representation of a real number as a bi-infinite sequence of integers. As in
[
Kar96
]
, we represent a real number
r
as a bi-infinite sturmian or periodic sequence of integer symbols
\lfloor r\rfloor
and
\lceil r\rceil
with well-defined average
r
. More precisely, for all
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, the
i
’th element of the sequence is

bal_{i}(r)=\lfloor(i+1)r\rfloor-\lfloor ir\rfloor.

(8.1)

We call this the balanced representation of the number
r
, and
bal_{i}(r)
is the
i
’th digit of the representation.

In our default tiling
w
, the second coordinates
t
of the north and the south edges of a horizontal row of tiles form balanced representations of real numbers. If
r
is the number represented by south edges then the north edges represent the number
r+\sigma_{1}
if there is a horizontal stripe in the tiles of the row, and
r+\tau_{1}
if there is no horizontal stripe, where

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\sigma_{1}&=&(1-\alpha)B_{1}+\alpha D_{1},\\
\tau_{1}&=&(1-\alpha)A_{1}+\alpha C_{1}.\end{array}

(8.2)

Numbers
\sigma_{1}
and
\tau_{1}
are chosen this way because they satisfy

\sigma_{1}\cdot\beta+\tau_{1}\cdot(1-\beta)=0.

(8.3)

This relation follows directly from (
1.1
) and (
8.2
).

The second coordinates
t
of the vertical edges are “carry” symbols to facilitate the representations. The construction is similar to the one in
[
Kar96
]
but, instead of multiplying real numbers by some rational constants, the tiles now add an irrational constant
\sigma_{1}
or
\tau_{1}
. Another difference is that the present tiles also do a similar addition symmetrically in the vertical direction: the third coordinates
u
of the east and the west edges of a vertical column form balanced representations of numbers, with corresponding “carries” given by the third components
u
of the north and the south edges. In this direction, the constants added when moving from a column to the next are

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\sigma_{2}&=&(1-\beta)C_{2}+\beta D_{2},\\
\tau_{2}&=&(1-\beta)A_{2}+\beta B_{2}.\end{array}

(8.4)

By (
1.1
) and (
8.4
) these numbers satisfy the relation

\sigma_{2}\cdot\alpha+\tau_{2}\cdot(1-\alpha)=0.

(8.5)

Let us pick any real numbers
y_{0}
and
z_{0}
as the numbers to be represented on the south edges of the horizontal row 0 and the west edges of the vertical column 0, respectively. Define, for every
j\in\mathbb{Z}
,

y_{j}=y_{0}+\sigma_{1}\cdot\lfloor j\beta\rfloor+\tau_{1}\cdot(j-\lfloor j\beta\rfloor).

(8.6)

Note that the right-hand-side evaluates to
y_{0}
when
j=0
, so that the notation is consistent. Note also that

y_{j+1}-y_{j}=\sigma_{1}\cdot bal_{j}(\beta)+\tau_{1}\cdot(1-bal_{j}(\beta))\\
=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sigma_{1},&\mbox{ if $bal_{j}(\beta)=1$,}\\
\tau_{1},&\mbox{ if $bal_{j}(\beta)=0$.}\end{array}\right.

(8.7)

In our default tiling
w
the south edges of the horizontal row
j
represent the number
y_{j}
,
and there is a horizontal stripe on row
j
if and only if
bal_{j}(\beta)=1
.

Analogously for the columns, we define, for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
,

z_{i}=z_{0}+\sigma_{2}\cdot\lfloor i\alpha\rfloor+\tau_{2}\cdot(i-\lfloor i\alpha\rfloor),

(8.8)

so that

z_{i+1}-z_{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\sigma_{2},&\mbox{ if $bal_{i}(\alpha)=1$,}\\
\tau_{2},&\mbox{ if $bal_{i}(\alpha)=0$.}\end{array}\right.

(8.9)

Let us next define the required “carry” symbols. For any
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
define

c_{i,j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\lfloor iy_{j}\rfloor-\lfloor iy_{j+1}\rfloor+\lfloor i\alpha\rfloor D_{1}+(i-\lfloor i\alpha\rfloor)B_{1},&\mbox{ if $bal_{j}(\beta)=1$,}\\
\lfloor iy_{j}\rfloor-\lfloor iy_{j+1}\rfloor+\lfloor i\alpha\rfloor C_{1}+(i-\lfloor i\alpha\rfloor)A_{1},&\mbox{ if $bal_{j}(\beta)=0$.}\\
\end{array}\right.

(8.10)

A direct calculation shows that, for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
,

c_{i+1,j}+bal_{i}(y_{j+1})-c_{i,j}-bal_{i}(y_{j})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}A_{1},&\mbox{ if $bal_{i}(\alpha)=0$ and $bal_{j}(\beta)=0$,}\\
B_{1},&\mbox{ if $bal_{i}(\alpha)=0$ and $bal_{j}(\beta)=1$,}\\
C_{1},&\mbox{ if $bal_{i}(\alpha)=1$ and $bal_{j}(\beta)=0$,}\\
D_{1},&\mbox{ if $bal_{i}(\alpha)=1$ and $bal_{j}(\beta)=1$,}\\
\end{array}\right.

(8.11)

Analogously, the carries
d_{i,j}
in the vertical direction are defined, for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
, by

d_{i,j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\lfloor jz_{i}\rfloor-\lfloor jz_{i+1}\rfloor+\lfloor j\beta\rfloor D_{2}+(j-\lfloor j\beta\rfloor)C_{2},&\mbox{ if $bal_{i}(\alpha)=1$,}\\
\lfloor jz_{i}\rfloor-\lfloor jz_{i+1}\rfloor+\lfloor j\beta\rfloor B_{2}+(j-\lfloor j\beta\rfloor)A_{2},&\mbox{ if $bal_{i}(\alpha)=0$.}\\
\end{array}\right.

(8.12)

As above, a direct calculation gives

d_{i,j+1}+bal_{j}(z_{i+1})-d_{i,j}-bal_{j}(z_{i})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}A_{2},&\mbox{ if $bal_{i}(\alpha)=0$ and $bal_{j}(\beta)=0$,}\\
B_{2},&\mbox{ if $bal_{i}(\alpha)=0$ and $bal_{j}(\beta)=1$,}\\
C_{2},&\mbox{ if $bal_{i}(\alpha)=1$ and $bal_{j}(\beta)=0$,}\\
D_{2},&\mbox{ if $bal_{i}(\alpha)=1$ and $bal_{j}(\beta)=1$,}\\
\end{array}\right.

(8.13)

Now we are ready to define the tile set. We give the tiles in the matrix formalism, discussed in Section
4.1
, where the colors of the east, north, west and south edges of a tile are written, in this order, as the columns of a
3\times 4
matrix. The tile set contains, for every
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
, the Wang tile
t_{i,j}
with the matrix representation

M_{t_{i,j}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c}bal_{j}(\beta)&bal_{i}(\alpha)&bal_{j}(\beta)&bal_{i}(\alpha)\\
\hline\cr c_{i+1,j}&bal_{i}(y_{j+1})&c_{i,j}&bal_{i}(y_{j})\\
bal_{j}(z_{i+1})&d_{i,j+1}&bal_{j}(z_{i})&d_{i,j}\end{array}\right).

(8.14)

The tile
t_{i,j}
is to be placed in cell
(i,j)
in our default tiling
w
. This default tiling is valid because
\textsc{North}(t_{i,j})=\textsc{South}(t_{i,j+1})
and
\textsc{East}(t_{i,j})=\textsc{West}(t_{i+1,j})
for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
.

The first row encodes the presence of stripes.
It follows that in the default tiling
w
the density of vertical stripes is equal to
\alpha
because the stripes are at columns
i
with
bal_{i}(\alpha)=1
.
Similarly, there is a horizontal stripe on row
j
if and only if
bal_{j}(\beta)=1
,
so that the horizontal stripes have density
\beta
in
w
.

Because

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{smallmatrix}\right)M_{t_{i,j}}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
1\\
-1\\
-1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}c_{i+1,j}+bal_{i}(y_{j+1})-c_{i,j}-bal_{i}(y_{j})\\
d_{i,j+1}+bal_{j}(z_{i+1})-d_{i,j}-bal_{j}(z_{i})\end{array}\right),

it follows from (
8.11
) and (
8.13
) that
(
3.1
) holds. Thus,
\mathcal{T}
determines the quadrilateral
ABDC
.

Finally, we need to prove that we have only constructed a finite number of tiles. As all elements of matrices
M_{t_{i,j}}
are integers, it is enough to prove that the absolute values of the elements are bounded by some constant.

•

The first row elements
bal_{i}(\alpha)
and
bal_{j}(\beta)
are in
\{0,1\}
.

•

Elements
bal_{i}(y_{j})
, for
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
, are balanced digits of numbers
y_{j}
, and so it is enough to bound the numbers
y_{j}
. Erasing the floor functions in (
8.6
) gives an approximation

y^{\prime}_{j}=y_{0}+j(\sigma_{1}\cdot\beta+\tau_{1}\cdot(1-\beta))

of
y_{j}
. By (
8.3
) we have that
y^{\prime}_{j}=y_{0}
is a constant, independent of
j
. As the difference of
y_{j}
and
y^{\prime}_{j}
comes only from erasing the floor functions,
we have that

|y_{j}-y_{0}|\leq|\sigma_{1}|+|\tau_{1}|
, proving that the numbers
|y_{j}|
have a bound independent of
j
. More precisely,
y_{j}-y_{0}=(\tau_{1}-\sigma_{1})\left\{j\beta\right\}
where, for any
r\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote
\left\{r\right\}\in[0,1)
for the fractional part
r-\lfloor r\rfloor
of
r
.
An analogous reasoning shows that
bal_{j}(z_{i})
are bounded for
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
, and that in fact

z_{i}-z_{0}=(\tau_{2}-\sigma_{2})\left\{i\alpha\right\}
.

•

Finally, to bound
c_{i,j}
, for
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
, we use a similar calculation. Erasing the floor functions from the definition (
8.10
) of numbers
c_{i,j}
gives their approximations
c^{\prime}_{i,j}
. If

bal_{j}(\beta)=1
we have

c^{\prime}_{i,j}=i(y_{j}-y_{j+1}+\alpha D_{1}+(1-\alpha)B_{1})=i(y_{j}-y_{j+1}+\sigma_{1})=0,

where we used (
8.2
), and also (
8.7
) to observe that
y_{j}-y_{j+1}+\sigma_{1}=0
when
bal_{j}(\beta)=1
. Similarly, if
bal_{j}(\beta)=0
we have

c^{\prime}_{i,j}=i(y_{j}-y_{j+1}+\alpha C_{1}+(1-\alpha)A_{1})=i(y_{j}-y_{j+1}+\tau_{1})=0.

The change in
c_{i,j}
caused by removing the floor functions in (
8.10
) is at most
1+1+|D_{1}|+|B_{1}|
when

bal_{j}(\beta)=1
, and at most
1+1+|C_{1}|+|A_{1}|
when

bal_{j}(\beta)=0
. In any case, integers
c_{i,j}
are bounded, independent of
i,j
.
More precisely,
c_{i,j}=\left\{iy_{j+1}\right\}-\left\{iy_{j}\right\}+\left\{i\alpha\right\}(B_{1}-D_{1})
when
bal_{j}(\beta)=1

and
c_{i,j}=\left\{iy_{j+1}\right\}-\left\{iy_{j}\right\}+\left\{i\alpha\right\}(A_{1}-C_{1})
when
bal_{j}(\beta)=0
.
An analogous reasoning shows that also the integers
d_{i,j}
are bounded, with

d_{i,j}=\left\{jz_{i+1}\right\}-\left\{jz_{i}\right\}-\left\{j\beta\right\}(C_{2}-D_{2})
when
bal_{i}(\alpha)=1
and

d_{i,j}=\left\{jz_{i+1}\right\}-\left\{jz_{i}\right\}-\left\{j\beta\right\}(A_{2}-B_{2})
when
bal_{i}(\alpha)=0
.

∎

Remark 8.2
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be the tile set constructed in the proof of Theorem
8.1
. One may extend
\mathcal{T}

by adding more tiles represented by
3\times 4
integer matrices that satisfy (
3.1
), i.e., that determine the same quadrilateral ABDC as
\mathcal{T}
under the same functions
v
and
h
. The extended tile set still satisfies Theorem
8.1
.
It follows that one can effectively find a tile set in Theorem
8.1
: calculate bounds on the elements of the matrices (
8.14
) as discussed in the end of the proof, and take in the tile set all matrices with so bounded elements that satisfy (
3.1
). All tiles given by (
8.14
) for any
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
are guaranteed to be among the constructed tiles.
The tile set may contain tiles that are not given by (
8.14
) but the additional tiles do not break Theorem
8.1
.

In the following lemma, we provide explicit upper bounds on the cardinalities of the
horizontal and vertical color sets with and without stripes in the constructed
tile set
\mathcal{T}
.
For simplicity, we do the calculations with the choice
y_{0}=z_{0}=0
.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be the set of Wang tiles constructed in Theorem
8.1

appearing in the configuration defined with parameters
y_{0}=z_{0}=0
.
The cardinalities of the horizontal and vertical color sets
with and without stripes in
\mathcal{T}
satisfy

\begin{array}[]{lcl}|I_{0}|&\leq&(|A_{1}-C_{1}|+1)\lceil|R_{2}-S_{2}|+1\rceil,\\
|I_{1}|&\leq&(|B_{1}-D_{1}|+1)\lceil|R_{2}-S_{2}|+1\rceil,\\
|J_{0}|&\leq&(|A_{2}-B_{2}|+1)\lceil|P_{1}-Q_{1}|+1\rceil,\\
|J_{1}|&\leq&(|C_{2}-D_{2}|+1)\lceil|P_{1}-Q_{1}|+1\rceil,\end{array}

(8.15)

where
P=(1-\alpha)A+\alpha C
,
Q=(1-\alpha)B+\alpha D
,
R=(1-\beta)A+\beta B
and

S=(1-\beta)C+\beta D
are the points of the quadrilateral ABDC shown in Figure
2
.

Proof.

Because
y_{j}=(\tau_{1}-\sigma_{1})\left\{j\beta\right\}
we have that
y_{j}\in[0,\tau_{1}-\sigma_{1})
or
y_{j}\in(\tau_{1}-\sigma_{1},0]
, depending on the sign of
\tau_{1}-\sigma_{1}
. The balanced digits of any
r\in\mathbb{R}
are among
\lfloor r\rfloor
and
\lceil r\rceil
so that
bal_{i}(y_{j})
is an element of the set

\{0,\dots\lceil\tau_{1}-\sigma_{1}\rceil\}
or the set
\{-\lceil\sigma_{1}-\tau_{1}\rceil,\dots,0\}
. In any case, there are at most
\lceil|\tau_{1}-\sigma_{1}|\rceil+1
possible symbols
bal_{i}(y_{j})
. Using (
8.2
) this number can also be written as

\lceil|(1-\alpha)A_{1}+\alpha C_{1}-(1-\alpha)B_{1}-\alpha D_{1})|+1\rceil=\lceil|P_{1}-Q_{1}|+1\rceil.

Analogously, there are at most

\lceil|\tau_{2}-\sigma_{2}|\rceil+1=\lceil|(1-\beta)A_{2}+\beta B_{2}-(1-\beta)C_{2}-\beta D_{2})|+1\rceil=\lceil|R_{2}-S_{2}|+1\rceil

possible values of
bal_{j}(z_{i})
.

The number of possible values
c_{i,j}
depends on whether
bal_{j}(\beta)
is 0 or 1.
If
bal_{j}(\beta)=1
then
c_{i,j}=\left\{iy_{j+1}\right\}-\left\{iy_{j}\right\}+\left\{i\alpha\right\}(B_{1}-D_{1})

so that
c_{i,j}
is an integer in the open interval
(-1,1+B_{1}-D_{1})
or

(-(1+B_{1}-D_{1}),1)
, depending on the sign of
B_{1}-D_{1}
. In either case, the number of possible values is at most
|B_{1}-D_{1}|+1
. If
bal_{j}(\beta)=0
then there are at most

|A_{1}-C_{1}|+1
possible values of
c_{i,j}
. Analogously, the number of different values that
d_{i,j}
attains is bounded by
|C_{2}-D_{2}|+1
when
bal_{i}(\alpha)=1
and by
|A_{2}-B_{2}|+1
when

bal_{i}(\alpha)=0
. Because
bal_{j}(\beta)=1
and
bal_{i}(\alpha)=1
characterize the presence of horizontal and vertical stripes in the tile, respectively, we obtain the upper bounds on the cardinalities of the horizontal and vertical color sets given in (
8.15
).
∎

We may now prove Theorem
B
.

Theorem
B
.

Let
(\alpha,\beta)\in[0,1]^{2}

be a pair of elements of a quadratic number field
K

such that

\alpha
and
\beta
are both irrational or both rational.
There exists a finite set

\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{\square}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}

of Wang tiles with vertical and horizontal stripes
and there exist four non-collinear points
A,B,C,D\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}

such that

(i)

(\alpha,\beta)
is a solution to Equation (
1.1
),

(ii)

\mathcal{T}
determines the quadrilateral
ABDC

whose opposite sides are not parallel,

(iii)

{\cal T}
admits a tiling
w:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\longrightarrow{\cal T}

where vertical stripes (
i.e.
, tiles in
\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
) have density
\alpha
, and the horizontal stripes
(tiles in
\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
) have density
\beta
,
and

(iv)

in every valid tiling by
\mathcal{T}
, the horizontal and the
vertical densities of stripes exist and are solutions of
Equation (
1.1
).

In particular, if
\alpha
and
\beta
are irrational, then the tile set
\mathcal{T}
is aperiodic.

Proof.

(i) Let
\alpha,\beta\in[0,1]
be elements of a quadratic number field
K
.
From Lemma
6.3
,
there exist four non-collinear points
A,B,C,D\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that

(\alpha,\beta)
is a solution to Equation (
1.1
).

(ii) and (iii)
From Lemma
6.3
,
we can choose
A,B,C,D
so that opposite sides of
ABDC
are not parallel
since
\alpha
and
\beta
are both irrational or both rational.
From Theorem
8.1
,
there exists a finite set

\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{\square}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}

of Wang tiles with horizontal and vertical stripes such that

\mathcal{T}
admits a tiling
w:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\longrightarrow{\cal T}
where horizontal
stripes (
i.e.
, tiles in
\mathcal{T}_{\boxminus}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
) have
density
\beta
, and the vertical stripes (tiles in
\mathcal{T}_{\boxbar}\cup\mathcal{T}_{\boxplus}
) have density
\alpha
, and

\mathcal{T}
determines the quadrilateral
ABDC
.

(iv)
Since the opposite sides of
ABDC
are not parallel,
we deduce from Proposition
6.2

that Equation (
1.1
) has finitely many solutions.
From Proposition
7.1
,
we deduce that in every valid tiling by
\mathcal{T}
, the horizontal and the
vertical densities of stripes exist and are solutions to Equation (
1.1
).

If
\beta
and
\alpha
are irrational, then
we deduce from Proposition
6.2

that every solution to Equation (
1.1
) is irrational.
Therefore, every valid configuration
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\mathcal{T}

is non-periodic since it must have
irrational densities of vertical and horizontal stripes.
From (iii), the set
\mathcal{T}
admits a valid configuration, so we
conclude that the tile set
\mathcal{T}
is aperiodic.
∎

Remark 8.4
.

Note that if exactly one of
\alpha
and
\beta
is rational, then the
number of solutions is infinite.
However, we believe that Proposition
7.1

can be modified to show that the stripe density corresponding to the rational
value exists and is equal to it.

Example 8.5
.

Let
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}&B_{1}&C_{1}&D_{1}\\
A_{2}&B_{2}&C_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0&1&-1\\
1&1&0&-1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
By Proposition
6.2
,
Equation (
1.1
) has two solutions.
In fact, (
1.1
) is equivalent to

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\beta&=&\alpha\\
\alpha^{2}+\alpha-1&=&0.\end{array}\right.

Only the solution

\alpha=\beta=\varphi^{-1}=\frac{\sqrt{5}-1}{2}

satisfies
\alpha,\beta\in[0,1]
. Let us construct a tile set
\mathcal{T}
that satisfies Theorem
8.1
.
From (
8.2
) and (
8.4
) we obtain

\begin{array}[]{rclcl}\sigma_{1}&=&\sigma_{2}&=&-\varphi^{-1},\\
\tau_{1}&=&\tau_{2}&=&1,\end{array}

so that
\tau_{1}-\sigma_{1}=\tau_{2}-\sigma_{2}=1+\varphi^{-1}=\varphi
.
Using the choice
y_{0}=0
we have, for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, that

y_{j}=(\tau_{1}-\sigma_{1})\left\{j\beta\right\}=\varphi\left\{j\beta\right\}\in[0,\varphi).

This
means that
bal_{i}(y_{j})\in\{0,1,2\}
for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Similarly, choosing
z_{0}=0
gives
bal_{j}(z_{i})\in\{0,1,2\}
for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
.

The calculations bounding
c_{i,j}
at the end of the proof of Theorem
8.1
show that

c_{i,j}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left\{iy_{j+1}\right\}-\left\{iy_{j}\right\}+\left\{i\alpha\right\}(B_{1}-D_{1})=\left\{iy_{j+1}\right\}-\left\{iy_{j}\right\}+\left\{i\alpha\right\}\in(-1,2),&\mbox{ when $bal_{j}(\beta)=1$},\\
\left\{iy_{j+1}\right\}-\left\{iy_{j}\right\}+\left\{i\alpha\right\}(A_{1}-C_{1})=\left\{iy_{j+1}\right\}-\left\{iy_{j}\right\}\in(-1,1),&\mbox{ when $bal_{j}(\beta)=0$},\\
\end{array}\right.

so that
c_{i,j}\in\{0,1\}
if
bal_{j}(\beta)=1
and
c_{i,j}\in\{0\}
if
bal_{j}(\beta)=0
.
Similarly,
d_{i,j}\in\{0,1\}
when
bal_{i}(\alpha)=1
and
d_{i,j}\in\{0\}
when
bal_{i}(\alpha)=0
.

All this combined, we have the following vertical and horizontal color sets, with and without stripes:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}I_{0}&=&\{0\}\times\{0\}\times\{0,1,2\},\\
I_{1}&=&\{1\}\times\{0\}\times\{0,1,2\}\ \cup\ \{1\}\times\{1\}\times\{0,1,2\},\\
J_{0}&=&\{0\}\times\{0,1,2\}\times\{0\},\\
J_{1}&=&\{1\}\times\{0,1,2\}\times\{0\}\ \cup\ \{1\}\times\{0,1,2\}\times\{1\}.\\
\end{array}

So there are nine horizontal colors and nine vertical colors. Taking all tiles with these colors and satisfying (
3.1
) yields an aperiodic Wang tile set with 108 tiles. As discussed in Remark
8.2
, not all these tiles may be necessary. In fact, computer experiments seem to indicate that only 80 of these 108 tiles come up from (
8.14
) for any
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
.

9
Open questions

We finish this article with some open questions.
First, one may wonder if statement (iv) of
Theorem
B

can be improved. More precisely, we may ask the following question.

Question 9.1
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a set of Wang tile constructed
in Theorem
B

from some pair
(\alpha,\beta)
of quadratic numbers
using Theorem
8.1
.
Is it true that for every configuration
w\in\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
,
the vertical and the horizontal densities of stripes exist and are equal to

\alpha
and
\beta
, respectively?

For instance, when

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A_{1}&B_{1}&C_{1}&D_{1}\\
A_{2}&B_{2}&C_{2}&D_{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1&0&-3\\
3&0&-1&-3\end{smallmatrix}\right)
,
Equation (
1.1
) has two solutions in
[0,1]^{2}
:

(\alpha,\beta)\approx(0.361325,0.589197)\quad\text{ and }\quad(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\approx(0.638675,0.18858).

Let
\mathcal{T}
be the tile set constructed as in Theorem
8.1
from the solution
(\alpha,\beta)
.
From Theorem
8.1
, we know there exists a configuration in
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
such that
the densities of vertical and horizontal stripes is
(\alpha,\beta)
.
But, is it possible to show that
for every
configuration in
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
,
the densities of vertical and horizontal stripes is
(\alpha,\beta)
?
From Proposition
7.1
,
we know that for every configuration in
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
,
the densities of vertical and horizontal stripes is a solution to the equation.
So, one only needs to rule out the possibility of having densities of vertical
and horizontal stripes equal to the other solution
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
.

Of course, to achieve that, Theorem
8.1

will need to become stronger, maybe
providing additional information about the tile set
involving
\sigma_{1}
,
\tau_{1}
,
\sigma_{2}
and
\tau_{2}
.
Because the union
\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is also a tile set which determines the same
quadrilateral
ABDC
if

\mathcal{T}
is the tile set generated from
(\alpha,\beta)

and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
is the tile set generated from
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
.
And there are configurations in the Wang shift

\Omega_{\mathcal{T}\cup\mathcal{T}^{\prime}}
with both possible values of stripe densities.
Bounding the elements of the matrices (
8.14
) is not sufficient to distinguish

\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
and, in fact, the tile sets constructed effectively for
(\alpha,\beta)
and
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})
in Remark
8.2
are identical.

A more general question is about the Wang shifts generated by these tile sets.
In
[
Lab25a
]
, minimality of the metallic mean Wang shifts was proved
using a criterion based on its self-similarity given as a 2-dimensional
rectangular substitution.
Can this method be used or extended to answer the following question?

Question 9.2
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a set of Wang tiles constructed
in Theorem
B

from some pair of quadratic numbers
using Theorem
8.1
.
Is the Wang shift
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
minimal as a dynamical system?

Note that a positive answer to Question
9.2
implies
that Question
9.1
also has a positive answer.

For Question
9.2
,
the first subcases to consider are the solutions
(\alpha,\beta)
for which the
associated Wang shift is expected to be self-similar, as in the case of
metallic mean Wang tiles.
Recall that a quadratic surd is said to be
reduced
if
\zeta
> 1 and its conjugate
\zeta^{\prime}

satisfies the inequalities
-1<\zeta^{\prime}<0
.
Galois proved that if
\zeta
is a reduced quadratic surd,
then the continued fractions for
\zeta
and for
(-1/\zeta^{\prime})
are both purely periodic.

Question 9.3
.

Let
\alpha,\beta
be two reduced quadratic surds in the same quadratic number field
and
\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}
respectively be their Galois algebraic conjugates.
Let
\mathcal{T}
be a set of Wang tiles constructed
in Theorem
B

from the pair
(-\alpha^{\prime},-\beta^{\prime})\in[0,1]^{2}

using Theorem
8.1
.
Is the Wang shift
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}
self-similar?
In other words,
does there exist an expansive 2-dimensional substitution
\omega:\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}\to\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}

such that
\Omega_{\mathcal{T}}=\overline{\omega(\Omega_{\mathcal{T}})}^{\sigma}
?
